\{v~:~X(v)-X^{\prime}(v)=s\}
is dense inside
J|_{a}
,
such that this event has significant probability
and conditioned on it the shift-partition inside
J|_{a}

is large, where both these facts are measured with respect to
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
. We give a formal definition of
P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})
in Lemma
3.9
.

Finally, we run the procedure Condition&Round using either the symmetrized versions of
\mu_{1}
or
\mu_{2}
which
returns a good assignment to the given subcube provided that the previous steps succeeded.

We now move on to the formal description of SubRound.

Algorithm 3.8

(SubRound: Rounding for a subgraph)
.

Input:
Takes as input an affine Unique-Games instance
I=(G,\Pi,\Sigma)
over an
(n,\ell,\alpha\ell)
-Johnson graph
G
and alphabet
\Sigma
, a pseudo-distribution
\mu
with
\mathrm{val}_{\mu}(I)\geq 1-\eta
, a
j
-restricted subcube
J|_{a}
, with
j\leq r=\left\lfloor\frac{32\sqrt{\eta}}{\alpha}\right\rfloor
.

Output:
Returns an assignment in
\Sigma^{|V(J|_{a})|}
to the vertices of
J|_{a}
.

1.

Fix
t=\frac{|\Sigma|^{3}\ell^{\Theta(r)}}{\eta^{3}},\tau=\Theta(\frac{\eta}{|\Sigma|^{2}\ell^{2r}\exp(r)}),\beta=\sqrt{\eta},\nu=\eta\exp(-r)
.

2.

Find a polynomial
P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})
from the set of polynomials given by Lemma
3.9
, subsets
A,B\subseteq V(J|_{a})
of size at most
t
and strings
y_{A},y_{B}
so that conditioning
\mu
on the events
Y_{A}=y_{A}
and
Y_{B}=y_{B}
gives pseudodistributions
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
satisfying:

(a)

Shift partition potential inside
J|_{a}
is significant:

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[\Phi^{a}_{\beta,\nu}(X,X^{\prime})P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})]\geq\exp(-r)\pE[P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})].

(b)

The probability of
P_{a}
is significant:

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})]\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\sqrt{\eta}}{|\Sigma|\ell^{j}\exp(r)}\right).

(c)

The random variables
Y_{u,v}
have small global correlation inside
J|_{a}
:

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},v_{1}\sim S:u_{1}\neq v_{1}\\
u_{2},v_{2}\sim S:u_{2}\neq v_{2}\end{subarray}}[I(Y_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y_{u_{2},v_{2}})]\leq\tau.

(d)

The random variables
Y_{u,v}^{\prime}
have small global correlation inside
J|_{a}
:

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},v_{1}\sim S:u_{1}\neq v_{1}\\
u_{2},v_{2}\sim S:u_{2}\neq v_{2}\end{subarray}}[I(Y^{\prime}_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y^{\prime}_{u_{2},v_{2}})]\leq\tau.

Here
S=V(J|_{a})
, and the mutual information is taken with respect to the collection of local distributions
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu})
. If no such polynomial or conditioning exists then output the all zeros assignment on
V(J|_{a})
and exit.

3.

Symmetrize
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
using the procedure in Section
2.4.3
to obtain
\mu^{\text{sym}}_{1},\mu^{\text{sym}}_{2}
. Perform derandomized Condition&Round on
\mu^{\text{sym}}_{1}
or
\mu^{\text{sym}}_{2}
and output the higher valued assignment obtained for
V(J|_{a})
.

3.2
Analysis of Algorithm
3.4

3.2.1
High Level Description of the Analysis

We prove that Algorithm
3.4
returns a solution with value independent of the alphabet size. We begin by explaining the ideas of the analysis
[
BBK+21
]
for small-set expanders, and then
explain how this analysis is adapted to our case.

Small-set expanders
[
BBK+21
]
:

Consider the shift-partition potential,
and suppose for simplicity of presentation that the
p
’s are replaced with the actual indicator functions.
Then the functions
F_{s}
cover
almost all the vertices of the graph,
and any edge that goes across parts in the shift-partition must be violated by either
X
or
X^{\prime}
. Hence if
X,X^{\prime}
were
(1-{\varepsilon})
-satisfying assignments then the shift-partition is non-expanding, in the sense that at most
2{\varepsilon}
-fraction of the edges go across the parts (those that are violated
by either
X
or
X^{\prime}
).

In the case of
(2{\varepsilon},\delta)
-small-set expanders,
[
BBK+21
]
then conclude that the fractional-size of one of the parts must be at least
\Omega(\delta)
, and in fact the shift-partition size:
\sum_{s}\E_{u\sim G}[F_{s}(u)]^{2}
, must be
\geq\Omega(\delta)
. On the other hand they prove that if the shift-partition potential is large on
\mu\times\mu
then Condition&Round on
\mu
succeeds in rounding to an
\Omega_{{\varepsilon},\delta}(1)
-assignment,
and formally they show:

1.

Given a degree
D
certificate of
(O({\varepsilon}),\delta)
-small-set expansion for a graph
G
and a degree
\Omega(D)
pseudodistribution
\mu
with value
1-{\varepsilon}
on any affine UG instance
I=(G,\Pi)
, conclude that
\Phi^{G}_{\beta,\nu}(\mu\times\mu)\geq\delta
.

2.

Show that if
\Phi^{G}_{\beta,\nu}(\mu\times\mu)
is large then Condition&Round outputs an assignment with large value.

Our analysis for Johnson graphs:

Let us see how to adapt this analysis to the case of Johnson graphs. Step (1) is far from being true since Johnson graphs are not small-set expanders. But we know that all non-expanding sets in these graphs must have large size when restricted to some subcube, although the set might not be large in the whole graph. We use this property to conclude that the shift-partition potential must be large on a subcube. This part of the analysis turns out to be much more non-trivial than the certifiable-SSE case and is where the bulk of our technical work lies.

Once we have this we use a suitably modified version of step (2) to conclude that Condition&Round when applied to the subcube outputs an
\Omega(1)
-assignment to the subcube. We then use an iteration lemma to run this algorithm multiple times to output an
\Omega(1)
-satisfying assignment to the whole graph.

3.2.2
Lemmas to be Proven Later

In this section, we state a few lemmas that are necessary for the analysis of
Algorithm
3.4
, whose proofs are deferred to later sections. In Section
3.2.3
we
use these lemmas to analyze the performance
of Algorithm
3.4
.

The analysis of Algorithm
3.4
proceeds by showing that given a pseudodistribution for
I
with high value, there exists a subcube with high SubRound value, i.e. showing the success of Step 3(a). To do so, we first prove that the structure theorem for Johnson graphs (Theorem
3.17
) implies that given a pseudodistribution
\mu
with large value, there exists a subcube
J|_{a}
with large shift-partition potential denoted by
\Phi^{a}_{\beta,\nu}
. Since the Johnson graph is not a small-set expander though, we have the following more subtle statement: There exists an
r=O(\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}/\alpha)
-restricted subcube
a
and an event
P_{a}
that has large probability, such that if we condition
\mu\times\mu
on
P_{a}
, the induced shift-partition potential is large on the subcube
J|_{a}
. The event
P_{a}
roughly corresponds to the indicator that the size of one of the shift-partition components is large inside the subcube
J|_{a}
, i.e.
\Ind(\delta(F_{s}|_{a})>\exp(-r))
and has probability
\geq\ell^{-O(r)}
.

Lemma 3.9
.

There exists a constant
{\varepsilon}_{0}\in(0,1)
, such that for all positive constants
{\varepsilon}\leq{\varepsilon}_{0}
,
\alpha,\tau\leq 1
,
\nu\leq{\varepsilon}\exp(-r)
, all integers
\ell\geq\Omega(r),n\geq\ell
, where
r=\lfloor\frac{32\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}{\alpha}\rfloor
the following holds.

Let
I
be an affine UG instance on
J(n,\ell,\alpha\ell)
and
\mu
be a pseudodistribution over assignments for
I
with
\mathrm{val}_{\mu}(I)\geq 1-{\varepsilon}
and degree at least
D=\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{|\Sigma|\ell^{r}\exp(r)}{\nu\tau\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}\right)
. Then there exists a restriction
a\subseteq[n]
of size
j\leq r
, a degree
\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)
polynomial
P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})
from a fixed set of
|\Sigma|
polynomials, subsets
A,B\subseteq V(J|_{a})
of size at most
\widetilde{O}(\frac{|\Sigma|\ell^{j}\exp(r)}{\tau\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}})
and strings
y_{A},y_{B}
such that conditioning
\mu
on the events
Y_{A}=y_{A}
and
Y_{B}=y_{B}
gives degree
\widetilde{\Omega}(1/\nu)
pseudodistributions
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
such that:

1.

\mathcal{A}_{I}~~\vdash_{\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)}~~P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})\in[0,1]
.

2.

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[\Phi^{a}_{\sqrt{{\varepsilon}},\nu}(X,X^{\prime})P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})]\geq\exp(-r)\pE[P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})]
.

3.

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})]\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}{|\Sigma|\ell^{j}\exp(r)}\right)
.

4.

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},v_{1}\sim S:u_{1}\neq v_{1}\\
u_{2},v_{2}\sim S:u_{2}\neq v_{2}\end{subarray}}[I(Y_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y_{u_{2},v_{2}})]\leq\tau.

5.

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},v_{1}\sim S:u_{1}\neq v_{1}\\
u_{2},v_{2}\sim S:u_{2}\neq v_{2}\end{subarray}}[I(Y^{\prime}_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y^{\prime}_{u_{2},v_{2}})]\leq\tau,

where
S=V(J|_{a})
,
Y_{u,v}=(X_{u},X_{v},p_{u},p_{v})
,
Y^{\prime}_{u,v}=(X^{\prime}_{u},X^{\prime}_{v},p^{\prime}_{u},p^{\prime}_{v})
and the mutual information is taken with respect to the collection of local distributions
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\sqrt{{\varepsilon}},\nu})
.

The next lemma captures the intuition that
given a collection of random variables
Z_{u,v}
that are roughly pairwise independent (that are functions of assignments
X
and
X^{\prime}
), and an event
E(X,X^{\prime})
with significant
probability, then typically
Z_{u,v}~|~E

is close to
Z_{u,v}
in statistical distance. Informally, this asserts that
conditioning on the event
E
does not
change the distributions of
(X,X^{\prime})
locally.

Lemma 3.10
.

For all
\tau,p,\beta,\nu,\delta\in(0,1)
, integers
D,N
,
m\leq N
the following holds: Let
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
be a degree
D+\widetilde{\Omega}(1/\nu)
pseudodistribution over
(X,X^{\prime})
satisfying
\mathcal{A}_{I}(X,X^{\prime})
and
E(X,X^{\prime})
be a polynomial such that,
\mathcal{A}_{I}\vdash_{D}E(X,X^{\prime})\in[0,1]
and
\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[E(X,X^{\prime})]\geq p
. Suppose for
S\subseteq[N]
we have that,

1.

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},v_{1}\sim S:u_{1}\neq v_{1}\\
u_{2},v_{2}\sim S:u_{2}\neq v_{2}\end{subarray}}[I(Y_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y_{u_{2},v_{2}})]\leq\tau.

2.

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},v_{1}\sim S:u_{1}\neq v_{1}\\
u_{2},v_{2}\sim S:u_{2}\neq v_{2}\end{subarray}}[I(Y^{\prime}_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y^{\prime}_{u_{2},v_{2}})]\leq\tau
,

where
Y_{u,v}=(X_{u},X_{v},p_{u},p_{v}),Y^{\prime}_{u,v}=(X^{\prime}_{u},X^{\prime}_{v},p^{\prime}_{u},p^{\prime}_{v})
and the mutual information is with respect to the collection of local distributions
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu})
(Definition
3.5
). Then we have that,

\Pr_{u,v\sim S:u\neq v}[TV((Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})|E,(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v}))\geq\delta]\leq O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\tau}+1/|S|}{p\delta^{2}}\right),

where the distribution
(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})|E
refers to the joint distribution on these variables defined by the collection of local distributions
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|E,p_{\beta,\nu})
and similarly
(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})
refers to the distribution defined by
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu})
.

We now restrict our attention to the nice subcube
J|_{a}
obtained from Lemma
3.9
and we henceforth only care about the relevant pseudodistributions (e.g.
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|P_{a}
) when marginalized to
J|_{a}
. For simplicity of notation we still refer to the marginalized pseudodistributions using their original notation.

We show that large shift-potential
\Phi^{a}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|P_{a})\geq\delta
with the additional property that an average
(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})
-pair when drawn from
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|P_{a}
is close to its distribution when drawn from
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
, implies that Condition&Round (applied on
J|_{a}
) succeeds on either
\mu_{1}
or
\mu_{2}
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
I=(G,\Pi)
be an affine instance of Unique Games over the alphabet
\Sigma
and
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
be a degree
D+\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)
pseudodistribution over assignments
(X,X^{\prime})
to
I
. Let
E(X,X^{\prime})
be a polynomial such that
\mathcal{A}_{I}(X,X^{\prime})\vdash_{D}E(X,X^{\prime})\in[0,1]
. Suppose we have that:

\Pr_{u,v\sim V(G):u\neq v}[TV((Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})|E,(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v}))>\delta]\leq\zeta,

where the distribution
(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})|E
refers to the joint distribution on these variables defined by the collection of local distributions
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|E,p_{\beta,\nu})
and similarly
(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})
refers to the distribution defined by
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu})
(Definition
3.5
).

If
\Phi^{G}_{\beta,\nu}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|E(X,X^{\prime}))\geq\gamma
, then on at least one of the pseudodistributions
\mu^{\text{sym}}_{1}
or
\mu^{\text{sym}}_{2}
Algorithm
3.7
runs in time
\mathrm{poly}(|V(G)|)
and returns an assignment of expected value at least

(\beta-\nu)^{2}\left(\gamma-O(\delta+\zeta)-\frac{1}{|V(G)|}\right)-3\nu(\beta-\nu)

for
I
.

Using the lemmas above for
G=J|_{a}
one can analyze a single iteration of SubRound and show that it manages to assign a subcube of
I
and within it satisfy a constant fraction of the edges. This subcube might be of size
o(1)
of the whole graph though, therefore to complete the analysis of our algorithm we need the following lemma, asserting that we can iterate this procedure (as done in Algorithm
3.4
) to satisfy a constant fraction of constraints of
I
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
c,\gamma,\delta\in(0,1]
and
r\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
I
be an affine UG instance on alphabet
\Sigma
on
J_{n,\ell,\alpha}
with
\ell,n
large enough and value at least
c
.
Suppose we have a subroutine
\mathcal{A}
which given as input any affine UG instance
I^{\prime}
on
J_{n,\ell,\alpha}
with
\mathrm{val}_{\mu}(I^{\prime})\geq c-\gamma
, returns an
\leq r
-restricted subcube
H
on
J
and a partial assignment
f
such that,
\text{val}_{f}(H)\geq\delta
.
Then if
\mathcal{A}
runs in time
T(\mathcal{A})
, there is a
O(|V(J)|T(\mathcal{A})+|V(J)|^{3})
-time algorithm which finds a solution for
I
that satisfies an
\Omega(\delta\gamma(1-\alpha)^{r})
-fraction of the edges of
J
.

Proof.

Deferred to Section
A
.
∎

3.2.3
The Analysis of Algorithm
3.4

Theorem 3.13
.

There exists a constant
{\varepsilon}_{0}\in(0,1)
, such that for all positive constants
{\varepsilon}\leq{\varepsilon}_{0}
,
\alpha\in{\mathbb{Q}}
with
\alpha\leq 1/2
, all integers
\ell\geq\Omega(r)
with
\alpha\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
r=\Theta(\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}/\alpha)
and
n
large enough, Algorithm
3.4
has the following guarantee: If
I
is an instance of affine Unique Games on the
(n,\ell,\alpha)
-Johnson graph
G
with alphabet
\Sigma
and
\mathrm{val}(I)=1-{\varepsilon}
, then in time
|V(J)|^{\mathrm{poly}(\ell^{r},|\Sigma|,1/{\varepsilon})}

Algorithm
3.4
returns an
\frac{\mathrm{poly}({\varepsilon})}{\exp(r)}
-satisfying assignment for
I
.

Proof.

Let
r=\lfloor 32{\varepsilon}/\alpha\rfloor
. Given the
\leq r
-restricted subcube
a\subseteq[n],P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})
and
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
from Lemma
3.9
we have that
\Phi_{\sqrt{{\varepsilon}},\nu}^{a}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|P_{a})\geq\exp(-r)
, therefore we apply Lemma
3.10
with
S=J|_{a}
, the polynomial
E(X,X^{\prime})=P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})
and the parameters
\beta=\sqrt{{\varepsilon}},\nu=\exp(-r){\varepsilon},\delta=\exp(-r),p=\Omega\left(\frac{\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}{|\Sigma|\ell^{r}\exp(r)}\right)
and
\tau=\Theta(\frac{{\varepsilon}}{|\Sigma|^{2}\ell^{2r}\exp(r)})
. The parameter
\tau
has been chosen so that
O(\frac{\sqrt{\tau}+1/|S|}{p\delta^{2}})=\exp(-r)
and therefore we can apply the rounding lemma (Lemma
3.11
) with
\zeta=\exp(-r)
and
\gamma=\exp(-r)
and the same settings of
\beta,\nu,\delta
. This shows that there exists a subcube
a\in{[n]\choose j}
with SubRound value that is at least
\Omega({\varepsilon}/\exp(r))
if
\deg(\mu)\geq\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{|\Sigma|\ell^{r}\exp(r)}{\nu\tau\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}\right)
. Hence it suffices to have degree of
\mu
equal to
\frac{|\Sigma|^{3}\ell^{O(r)}}{{\varepsilon}^{3}}
.

By using SubRound as a subroutine, we finish the proof of this theorem by applying the iteration Lemma
3.12
with
c=1-{\varepsilon}
,
\gamma={\varepsilon}
,
\delta=\Omega({\varepsilon}/\exp(r))
and
r=\lfloor 64\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}/\alpha\rfloor
so that
(1-\alpha)^{r}=O(\sqrt{{\varepsilon}})
(Claim
3.21
). We can check that SubRound satisfies the hypotheses of the lemma: it finds a
\leq r
-restricted subcube and an assignment to it with value
\Omega(\delta)
in time
|V(J)|^{\frac{|\Sigma|^{3}\ell^{O(r)}}{{\varepsilon}^{3}}}
(this follows from the degree upper bound on
\mu
).
This gives us that Algorithm
3.4
outputs an assignment of value at least
\Omega({\varepsilon}^{3}/\exp(r))
in time
|V(G)|^{\frac{|\Sigma|^{3}\ell^{O(r)}}{{\varepsilon}^{3}}}
.
∎

3.3
Proof of Lemma
3.9
: Finding Subcube with Large Shift-partition

In this section we use the structure theorem for Johnson graphs
to prove the existence of a restriction
a\subseteq[n]
of constant size (corresponding to the induced subgraph
J|_{a}
) with large induced shift-partition potential when conditioned on a non-negative polynomial
P_{a}
, i.e.
\Phi^{a}_{\beta,\nu}(\mu\times\mu|P_{a})
is large. Recall that to analyze our final Condition&Round step though we need to show conditioning
\mu\times\mu
on
P_{a}
does not introduce too many correlations between
X,X^{\prime}
. Therefore we first perform a global correlation reduction procedure on
\mu\times\mu
to get the product pseudodistribution
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
while preserving the property that
\Phi^{a}_{\beta,\nu}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|P_{a})
is large.
The exact quantity that aids our rounding analysis is:

I(Y_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y_{u_{2},v_{2}})=I(X_{u_{1}},X_{v_{1}},p_{u_{1}},p_{v_{1}};X_{u_{2}},X_{v_{2}},p_{u_{2}},p_{v_{2}}),

defined according to the collection of local distributions in Definition
3.5
. We upper bound this by using the global correlation reduction procedure of Raghavendra-Tan
[
RT12
]
.

3.3.1
Restricting the Shift Partition to a Subgraph

To prove Lemma
3.9
we will need the notion of

global shift-partition potential restricted to subcube
J|_{a}
, which is almost the same as shift-partition potential on
J|_{a}
except for one key difference:

Definition 3.14

(Global shift-potential restricted to Subgraphs
[
BBK+21
]
)
.

For any
\nu,\beta\in(0,1)
and subgraph
H
of
G
, define the global shift-partition size restricted to the subgraph
H
to be the quantity:

\Phi_{\beta,\nu}(X,X^{\prime})|_{H}=\sum_{s\in\Sigma}\E_{u\in H}[F_{s}^{G}(u)]^{2},

where
F_{s}
’s are the functions defining the shift-partition (Definition
3.3
).
Let the global shift-partition potential with respect to a pseudodistribution
\mathcal{D}
over pairs of assignments
(X,X^{\prime})
to
G
be:

\Phi_{\beta,\nu}(\mathcal{D})|_{H}=\pE_{(X,X^{\prime})\sim\mathcal{D}}[\Phi_{\beta,\nu}(X,X^{\prime})|_{H}].

Note the difference between the global potential and the shift-partition potential on
H
: the global shift-partition potential
\Phi_{\beta,\nu}(X,X^{\prime})|_{H}
measures the size of the global partition inside
H
, i.e.
\mathrm{val}_{u}^{G}(X)
is a function of all the edges of the graph that are incident on
u
, not just the edges in the subgraph
H
. The potential

\Phi_{\beta,\nu}^{H}(X,X^{\prime})
, on
the other hand, measures the value of a vertex
u
only
inside
the subgraph
H
.

3.3.2
Global Hypercontractivity

We will need the following definition to describe the structure theorem for Johnson graphs:

Definition 3.15

(Restrictions of Functions)
.

For the
(n,\ell,\alpha)
-Johnson graph
J
, given a function
F:V(J)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and a set
a\subseteq[n]
with
a=r
, such that
0\leq r\leq\ell-1
, we define the restricted function
F|_{a}:\binom{[n]\setminus a}{\ell-r}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as,

F|_{a}(X)=F(a\cup X).

Further, let
\delta(F|_{a})
denote the fractional size of the function restricted to the subcube
J|_{a}
, that is,

\delta(F|_{a}):=\E\limits_{X\sim\binom{[n]\setminus a}{\ell-r}}[F|_{a}(X)].

When
a=\phi
and
r=0
, we have that
F|_{a}(X)=F(X)
for all
X\in\binom{[n]}{\ell}
and
\delta(F|_{a})=\delta(F)=\E[F]
.

In
[
KMMS18
]
it is shown that pseudorandom sets expand. Formally, we define pseudorandom sets and in general pseudorandom functions as follows:

Definition 3.16

(Pseudorandom functions)
.

A set
S
is called
(r,\gamma)
-pseudorandom if for all
\leq r
-restrictions
a
,
\delta(S|_{a})\leq\gamma
. Similarly a function
F
bounded in
[0,1]
is called
(r,\gamma)
-pseudorandom if for all
\leq r
-restrictions
a
,
\delta(F^{2}|_{a})\leq\gamma
.

We will need a version of this
result for general Boolean and pseudorandom functions
F
, and moreover to show that it is proved in the
SoS proof system of constant degree. We get an SoS proof that shows that if
\delta(F^{2}|_{a})\leq\gamma
for all
a
of size
\leq r
and
F
is Boolean, then
\langle F,LF\rangle
is large, with
q_{a}(F)
being the SoS multipliers of the axioms
\{\gamma-\delta(F^{2}|_{a})\}_{a}
and
(\Pi_{\geq\lambda_{r}}F)(X)
being the multiplier for the axioms
\{F(X)^{3}=F(X)\}
. We have the following statement:

Theorem 3.17

(Expansion Theorem for Johnson Graphs)
.

For all
\alpha\in(0,1)
, all integers
\ell\geq 1/\alpha
and
n\geq\ell
, the following holds: Let
J(n,\ell,\alpha\ell)
be the
\alpha
-noisy Johnson graph. For every constant
\gamma\in(0,1)
and positive integer
r\leq O(\ell)
, every function
F:V(J)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that is
(r,\gamma)
-pseudorandom has high expansion:

\displaystyle\{F(X)\in[0,1]\}_{X\in V(J)}\vdash_{O(1)}\,

\displaystyle\langle F,LF\rangle\geq\delta(F)(1-(1-\alpha)^{r+1})(1-\gamma^{1/3}\exp(r))-\sum_{j=0}^{r}\frac{c_{j}\ell^{j}}{\gamma}\E_{a\sim{[n]\choose j}}[q_{a}(F)({\delta(F^{2}|_{a})-\gamma)}]+B(F)

(4)

\displaystyle\{F(X)\in[0,1]\}\,\,\,\vdash_{2}\,\,\,0\leq q_{a}(F)\leq 1

(5)

where for all
j\leq r
,
c_{j}
’s are positive constants of size at most

\exp(r)
, for all size
j
subsets
a\subseteq[n]
,
q_{a}(F)
are degree
2
polynomials and
B(F)=\frac{4}{3}\E_{X}[(F^{3}-F)\Pi_{\geq\lambda_{r}}F]
for
\lambda_{r}=(1-\alpha)^{r}
and
\Pi_{\geq\lambda}
denoting the projection operator to the top-eigenspace of
J
of eigenvalues
\geq\lambda
.

Proof.

Deferred to Section
C
.
∎

One can equivalently view the expansion result in
[
KMMS18
]
as asserting that if
F
is a Boolean function which is the
indicator of a set
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\binom{[n]}{\ell}
whose edge
expansion is bounded away from
1
, then
\delta(F|_{a})\geq\Omega(1)
for
some
r
-restricted subcube
a
with constant
r
. In fact, if the expansion of
F
is at most
1-{\varepsilon}
on
J_{n,\ell,\alpha}
, then
\delta(F|_{a})\geq\exp(-r)
for
r=O({\varepsilon}/\alpha)
. Similarly, we can use the above SoS statement to conclude that even if
F
is “almost-Boolean” and non-expanding, then
q_{a}(F)(\delta(F^{2}|_{a})-\gamma)>0
for some
O(1)
-restriction
a
. In the context of a pseudodistribution
\mu
over non-expanding sets
F
, one can conclude that conditioning
\mu
on
q_{a}(F)
results in a new pseudodistribution
\mu^{\prime}
where
\delta(F^{2}|_{a})>\gamma
on average, and therefore
q_{a}(F)
roughly corresponds to the “event” that
F
is dense on
J|_{a}
.

We use the observation that the shift-partition defined with respect to
(1-{\varepsilon})
-satisfying assignments
X
and
X^{\prime}
has expansion at most
2{\varepsilon}
, therefore is non-expanding. Therefore as above, using the structure theorem we conclude that at least one of the sets in the shift-partition is not
(r,\gamma)
-pseudorandom for
r=O({\varepsilon}/\alpha)
and
\gamma=\exp(-r)
, i.e.
\delta(F_{s}|_{a})\geq\gamma
for some
s\in\Sigma
and
\leq r
-restriction
a
. Let
\mathrm{viol}(X)
denote the fraction of edges that an assignment
X
violates in the instance
I
. We frame this fact in SoS using the following lemma:

Lemma 3.18
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
3.9
, for all
\eta>0
and
r\leq\ell
we get:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{I}

\displaystyle\vdash_{\tilde{O}(1/\nu)}

(6)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{r}c_{j}\exp(r)\ell^{j}\E_{a\in{[n]\choose j}}[\sum_{s}q_{a}(F_{s})(\delta(F_{s}|_{a})-\exp(-r))]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1-\lambda_{r+1}}{2}-(\mathrm{viol}(X)+\mathrm{viol}(X^{\prime}))\left(1+\frac{3(1-\lambda_{r+1})+12+8\eta}{6(1-\beta-\nu)}\right)-\frac{2}{3\eta}-\nu\left(5-\lambda_{r+1}+\frac{8\eta}{3}\right),

(7)

where
\lambda_{r+1}=(1-\alpha)^{r+1}
,
q_{a}
are the polynomials in Theorem
3.17
,
\mathcal{A}_{I}
is the set of axioms defined for
I
by program (
1
).

Proof.

This proof proceeds exactly as the proof of Lemma 4.4 for certifiable small-set expanders in
[
BBK+21
]
:
we apply Theorem
3.17
to each function
F_{s}
and sum up over
s
. Doing so, we get the following inequality:

\displaystyle\sum_{s}\langle F_{s},LF_{s}\rangle\geq

\displaystyle(1-\lambda_{r+1})(1-\gamma^{1/3}\exp(r))\sum_{s}\delta(F_{s})-\sum_{j=0}^{r}\frac{c_{j}\ell^{j}}{\gamma}\E_{a\in[n]^{j}}[\sum_{s}q_{a}(F_{s})(\delta(F_{s}^{2}|_{a})-\gamma)]

\displaystyle+\frac{4}{3}\sum_{s}\E[(F_{s}^{3}-F_{s})\Pi_{\geq\lambda_{r}}F_{s}]

(8)

We now set
\gamma=\exp(-r)
such that
(1-\gamma^{1/3}\exp(r))=1/2
. Further we bound each of the terms
\sum_{s}\langle F_{s},LF_{s}\rangle,\sum_{s}\delta(F_{s})
and the Booleanity error:
\frac{4}{3}\sum_{s}\E[(F_{s}^{3}-F_{s})\Pi_{\geq\lambda_{r}}F_{s}]
.

First note that the fraction of low-valued vertices is small and in particular is at most
\frac{\mathrm{viol}(X)+\mathrm{viol}(X^{\prime})}{1-\beta}
. Since the functions cover all of the high-valued vertices we get
\sum\delta(F_{s})\gtrsim 1-\frac{\mathrm{viol}(X)+\mathrm{viol}(X^{\prime})}{1-\beta}
. Next,
\sum_{s}\langle F_{s},LF_{s}\rangle
counts the fraction of edges crossing the shift-partition. Every such edge must be violated by either
X
or
X^{\prime}
or must be incident on a vertex with low value, therefore we get:
\sum_{s}\langle F_{s},LF_{s}\rangle\lesssim\mathrm{viol}(X)+\mathrm{viol}(X^{\prime})+\frac{2\mathrm{viol}(X)+2\mathrm{viol}(X^{\prime})}{1-\beta}
. We have put approximate inequalities here since there are some error terms generated because
F_{s}
’s are not exact indicator functions.
The Booleanity term
B(F)
in Theorem
3.17
is also small (after summing up) because
F_{s}
’s are approximate-indicators (note that it is
0
for
0/1
functions since
F^{3}=F
)
12
12

12

Note that if the functions
F_{s}
were defined using
\Ind[\mathrm{val}_{u}(X)\geq\beta]
then we would obtain the lemma statement without the terms involving
\eta
and with
\nu=0
. These statements have been made formal in the claims from Section 4.2 of
[
BBK+21
]
, specifically Claim 4.2, 4.7 and 4.9 therein. They are simple to prove given the properties of the approximate-indicator polynomial
p_{\beta,\nu}
hence we omit them here.

Using the above claims to bound each sum, plugging in the bounds in (
8
) and rearranging we get the desired inequality.

∎

3.3.3
Reducing Global Correlation

The last ingredient we need for the proof of Lemma
3.9
is
that given a pseudo-expectation of sufficiently high degree, one can
construct different pseudo-expectations (which are conditionals
of the initial pseudo-expectation) that have no global correlations.
More precisely:

Lemma 3.19
.

For all
\tau,p,\beta,\nu\in(0,1)
and
|\Sigma|,D,D^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}

such that
D^{\prime}\geq\max(D,\widetilde{\Omega}(1/\nu))
the following holds.

Suppose there is a degree
D^{\prime}+\widetilde{\Omega}(\frac{\log|\Sigma|}{p\nu\tau})
pseudodistribution
\mu
over UG assignments that satisfies
\mathcal{A}_{I}
, and a polynomial
E(X,X^{\prime})
satisfying
\pE_{(X,X^{\prime})\sim\mu\times\mu}[E(X,X^{\prime})]\geq p
and
\mathcal{A}_{I}\vdash_{D}E(X,X^{\prime})\leq 1
. Then for all subsets
S\subseteq[N]
, there exist subsets
A,B\subseteq S
of size at most
O(\frac{\log|\Sigma|}{p\tau})
and strings
y_{A},y_{B}
such that conditioning
\mu
on the events
Y_{A}=y_{A}
and
Y_{B}=y_{B}
gives pseudodistributions
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
of degree at least
D^{\prime}
such that:

1.

\pE_{X,X^{\prime}\sim\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[E(X,X^{\prime})]\geq\frac{p}{2}
.

2.

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},v_{1}\sim S:u_{1}\neq v_{1}\\
u_{2},v_{2}\sim S:u_{2}\neq v_{2}\end{subarray}}[I(Y_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y_{u_{2},v_{2}})]\leq\tau.

3.

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},v_{1}\sim S:u_{1}\neq v_{1}\\
u_{2},v_{2}\sim S:u_{2}\neq v_{2}\end{subarray}}[I(Y^{\prime}_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y^{\prime}_{u_{2},v_{2}})]\leq\tau
,

where for
A\subseteq[N]
,
Y_{A}=(X_{i_{1}},\ldots,X_{i_{|A|}},p_{i_{1}},\ldots,p_{i_{|A|}})
(same for
Y^{\prime}
) and the mutual information is with respect to the collection of local distributions
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu})
.

Proof.

Deferred to Section
3.4
.
∎

3.3.4
Proof of Lemma
3.9

We combine the lemmas stated in the previous section and set parameters to complete the proof of Lemma
3.9
.

Lemma 3.20

(Restatement of Lemma
3.9
)
.

There exists a constant
{\varepsilon}_{0}\in(0,1)
, such that for all positive constants
{\varepsilon}\leq{\varepsilon}_{0}
,
\alpha,\tau\leq 1
,
\nu\leq{\varepsilon}\exp(-r)
, all integers
\ell\geq\Omega(r),n\geq\ell
, where
r=\lfloor\frac{32\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}{\alpha}\rfloor
the following holds: Let
I
be an affine UG instance on
J_{n,\ell,\alpha}
and
\mu
be a pseudodistribution over assignments for
I
with
\mathrm{val}_{\mu}(I)\geq 1-{\varepsilon}
and degree at least
\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{|\Sigma|\ell^{r}\exp(r)}{\nu\tau\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}\right)
. There exists a restriction
a\subseteq[n]
of size
j\leq r
, a degree
\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)
polynomial
P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})
in a fixed set of
|\Sigma|
polynomials, subsets
A,B\subseteq V(J|_{a})
of size at most
\widetilde{O}(\frac{|\Sigma|\ell^{j}\exp(r)}{\tau\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}})
and strings
y_{A},y_{B}
such that conditioning
\mu
on the events
Y_{A}=y_{A}
and
Y_{B}=y_{B}
gives degree
\widetilde{\Omega}(1/\nu)
pseudodistributions
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
such that:

1.

\mathcal{A}_{I}~~\vdash_{\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)}~~P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})\in[0,1]
.

2.

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[\Phi^{a}_{\sqrt{{\varepsilon}},\nu}(X,X^{\prime})P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})]\geq\exp(-r)\pE[P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})]
.

3.

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})]\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}{|\Sigma|\ell^{j}\exp(r)}\right)
.

4.

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},v_{1}\sim S:u_{1}\neq v_{1}\\
u_{2},v_{2}\sim S:u_{2}\neq v_{2}\end{subarray}}[I(Y_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y_{u_{2},v_{2}})]\leq\tau.

5.

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},v_{1}\sim S:u_{1}\neq v_{1}\\
u_{2},v_{2}\sim S:u_{2}\neq v_{2}\end{subarray}}[I(Y^{\prime}_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y^{\prime}_{u_{2},v_{2}})]\leq\tau,

where
S=V(J|_{a})
,
Y_{u,v}=(X_{u},X_{v},p_{u},p_{v})
,
Y^{\prime}_{u,v}=(X^{\prime}_{u},X^{\prime}_{v},p^{\prime}_{u},p^{\prime}_{v})
and the mutual information is taken with respect to the collection of local distributions
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\sqrt{{\varepsilon}},\nu})
.

Proof.

We will apply Lemma
3.18
with the following parameters:
r=\lfloor 32\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}/\alpha\rfloor
, so that
1-\lambda_{r+1}\geq 16\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}
,
\eta=1/\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}
and
\beta=201\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}
.
Since
{\varepsilon}
is sufficiently small and
\nu\leq{\varepsilon}/2
we get that
1/(1-\beta-\nu)<2
. By assumption
\pE_{\mu}[\mathrm{viol}(X)]=\pE_{\mu}[\mathrm{viol}(X^{\prime})]\leq{\varepsilon}
,
\pE_{\mu}
has degree
\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)
and
\pE_{\mu}
satisfies
\mathcal{A}_{I}
, therefore taking the pseudoexpectation of equation
7
with respect to the pseudodistribution
\mu\times\mu
we get:

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{r}c_{j}\exp(r)\ell^{j}\E_{a\in{[n]\choose j}}\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[\sum_{s}q_{a}(F_{s})(\delta(F_{s}|_{a})-\exp(-r))]\geq\frac{\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}{3}.

(9)

Since
c_{j}
’s are smaller than
\exp(r)
, an averaging argument gives us a size
j\leq r
restriction
a\subseteq[n]
and an
s\in\Sigma
such that:

\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[q_{a}(F_{s})(\delta(F_{s}|_{a})-\exp(-r))]\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}{|\Sigma|\ell^{j}\exp(r)}\right).

(10)

Let
P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})
be the polynomial
q_{a}(F_{s}(X,X^{\prime}))
from above, abbreviated henceforth as
P_{a}
. First note that since
q_{a}
is a degree
O(1)
-SoS polynomial we immediately get that
P_{a}
is a degree
\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)
SoS polynomial such that:

\mathcal{A}_{I}~~\vdash_{\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)}F_{s}(u)\in[0,1]~~\vdash_{O(1)}~~q_{a}(F_{s}(X,X^{\prime}))=P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})\in[0,1],

thus proving the first statement in the theorem.
We now reduce the global correlations using Lemma
3.19
.
Namely, we take
\mu
with
S
being the set of variables corresponding to the subcube
a
, the polynomial
E(X,X^{\prime})=P_{a}(\delta(F_{s}|_{a})-\exp(-r))
(for which we know that
\mathcal{A}\vdash_{\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)}E(X,X^{\prime})\leq 1
),
\beta=\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}
and
\tau,\nu
as in the lemma statement. Thus, we get a
pseudodistribution
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
over UG assignments to the subcube
a
, such that:

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P_{a}(\delta(F_{s}|_{a})-\exp(-r))]\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}{|\Sigma|\ell^{j}\exp(r)}\right),

(11)

along with the conditions on the mutual information of
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
.
This proves point (4),(5) from the lemma statement.

Establishing point (2) of the lemma.

Let us now derive the fact that the global shift-partition potential
\Phi(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2})|_{a}
is large conditioned on
P_{a}
.
Rearranging (
11
) we get that:

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P_{a}\delta(F_{s}|_{a})]\geq\exp(-r)\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P_{a}]+\Omega\left(\frac{\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}{|\Sigma|\ell^{j}\exp(r)}\right)\geq\exp(-r)\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P_{a}].

(12)

Since
P_{a}
is an SoS polynomial we can reweight
\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[\cdot]
by
P_{a}
to get
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}^{\prime}}
and apply Cauchy-Schwarz to get:

\exp(-r)\leq\widetilde{\mathbb{E}^{\prime}}[\delta(F_{s}|_{a})]\leq\sqrt{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}^{\prime}}[\delta(F_{s}|_{a})^{2}]}=\sqrt{\frac{\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[\delta(F_{s}|_{a})^{2}P_{a}]}{\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P_{a}]}},

rearranging which we get that:
\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[\delta(F_{s}|_{a})^{2}P_{a}]\geq\exp(-r)\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P_{a}]
. By definition, the global potential restricted to
a
is equal to
\sum_{s\in\Sigma}\delta(F_{s}|_{a})^{2}
. Therefore adding the terms
\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[\delta(F_{s^{\prime}}|_{a})^{2}P_{a}]
for
s^{\prime}\in\Sigma,s^{\prime}\neq s
(which are all non-negative) to the LHS of (
12
) we get:

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[\Phi_{\beta,\nu}(X,X^{\prime})|_{a}P_{a}]\geq\exp(-r)\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P_{a}].

(13)

We will now relate the global potential to the shift-partition potential on
a
, where the only difference between the quantities is that in the former the value of a vertex is the fraction of edges satisfied in the whole graph, whereas in the latter it is the value calculated according to only the edges inside the subcube
a
(in the terms
p_{\beta,\nu}(\mathrm{val}(u))
).

First note that that an
r
-restricted subcube has bounded expansion when
r
is not too large:
\phi(J|_{a})\leq O(\sqrt{{\varepsilon}})
(Claim
3.21
). Using this we get that,

\Phi^{a}_{\beta-200\sqrt{{\varepsilon}},\nu}(X,X^{\prime})\geq\Phi_{\beta,\nu}(X,X^{\prime})|_{a}-4\nu,

which we prove formally in Claim
3.22
.

Overall, we get:

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[\Phi^{a}_{\sqrt{{\varepsilon}},\nu}(X,X^{\prime})P_{a}]\geq\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[(\Phi_{201\sqrt{{\varepsilon}},\nu}(X,X^{\prime})|_{a}-4\nu)P_{a}]\geq\exp(-r)\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P_{a}],

since
\nu\leq\exp(-r)/8
, which establishes point (2) of the
lemma.

Point (3) follows from (
11
). Indeed,
since
\delta(F_{s}|_{a})\leq 1
and
P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})\geq 0
(both facts are certifiable in degree
\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)
) we get that:
\mathcal{A}_{I}\vdash_{\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)}P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})(\delta(F_{s}|_{a})-\exp(-r))]\leq(1-\exp(-r))P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})
. Taking
\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}
and plugging this into equation (
11
) we get that:

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P_{a}]\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}{|\Sigma|\ell^{j}\exp(r)}\right)\qed

3.3.5
Auxiliary Claims

We end this section by giving the proofs of Claims
3.21
and
3.22
. These were also used in
[
BBK+21
]
but we include the proofs here for completeness.

Claim 3.21

(Claim 6.10
[
BBK+21
]
)
.

If
r=\left\lfloor\frac{32\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}{\alpha}\right\rfloor<\frac{\ell}{4}
and
s<r
, an
s
-restricted subcube of
J(n,\ell,\alpha\ell)
has expansion at most
200\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}
.

Proof.

Let
J|_{Y}
be an
s
-restricted subcube.
We have that,

1-\phi(J|_{Y})=\frac{\binom{\ell-|Y|}{\alpha\ell}}{\binom{\ell}{\alpha\ell}}\geq\frac{\binom{\ell-r}{\alpha\ell}}{\binom{\ell}{\alpha\ell}}=\left(\frac{\ell-\alpha\ell}{\ell}\right)\left(\frac{\ell-\alpha\ell-1}{\ell-1}\right)\ldots\left(\frac{\ell-\alpha\ell-r+1}{\ell-r+1}\right).

Now since
r\leq\ell/4
by assumption, each of the parenthesized terms is at least
\left(\frac{3\ell/4-\alpha\ell}{3\ell/4}\right)=(1-4\alpha/3)
, so

1-\phi(J|_{Y})\geq\left(1-\frac{4\alpha}{3}\right)^{r}\geq 1-\frac{4r\alpha}{3}.

Since
r=\left\lfloor\frac{32\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}{\alpha}\right\rfloor<\frac{75\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}{\alpha}
, we get that
\phi(J|_{Y})<200\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}

as desired.
∎

Claim 3.22

(Claim 6.11
[
BBK+21
]
)
.

Suppose that
C
is an
r
-restricted subcube of
J_{n,\ell,\alpha}
with
r=\left\lfloor\frac{32\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}{\alpha}\right\rfloor
.
Then if
\Phi^{C}
is the shift-partition potential restricted to
C
, for any
\beta\geq 201\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}
and
\nu<{\varepsilon}
,

\Phi^{C}_{\beta-200\sqrt{{\varepsilon}},\nu}(X,X^{\prime})\geq\Phi_{\beta,\nu}(X,X^{\prime})|_{C}-4\nu,

and furthermore this is certifiable in degree
\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)
SoS.

Proof.

When
r=\left\lfloor\frac{32\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}{\alpha}\right\rfloor
, the expansion of
C
is at most
1-(1-4\alpha/3)^{r}\leq 200\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}
by Claim
3.21
.
Furthermore, from the definition of the Johnson graph this holds vertex-by-vertex; every
v\in C
has at most a
200\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}
-fraction of its neighbors outgoing.
Therefore,

\Ind[\mathrm{val}_{u}^{C}(X)\geq\beta-200\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}]\geq\Ind[\mathrm{val}_{u}(X)\geq\beta],

and furthermore since
\nu<{\varepsilon}
,

p_{\beta-200\sqrt{{\varepsilon}},\nu}(\mathrm{val}_{u}^{C}(X))+\nu\geq p_{\beta,\nu}(\mathrm{val}_{u}(X))-\nu.

The same holds for
p(\mathrm{val}_{u}(X^{\prime}))
. Let
Z_{u,s}=\Ind[X_{u}-X^{\prime}_{u}=s]
. Therefore, by definition,

\displaystyle\Phi_{\beta-200\sqrt{{\varepsilon}},\nu}(X|_{C},X^{\prime}|_{C})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\Sigma}\E_{u\in C}\left[Z_{u,s}\cdot p_{\beta-200\sqrt{{\varepsilon}},\nu}(\mathrm{val}_{u}^{C}(X))\cdot p_{\beta-200\sqrt{{\varepsilon}},\nu}(\mathrm{val}_{u}^{C}(X^{\prime}))\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{s\in\Sigma}\E_{u\in C}\left[Z_{u,s}\cdot(p_{\beta,\nu}(\mathrm{val}_{u}(X))-2\nu)\cdot(p_{\beta,\nu}(\mathrm{val}_{u}(X^{\prime}))-2\nu)\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\Phi_{\beta,\nu}(X,X^{\prime})|_{C}-4\nu,

where each inequality is a sum-of-squares inequality of degree at most
2\deg(p)
.
∎

3.4
Proof of Lemma
3.19
: Reducing Global Correlation

We will use the following lemma from
[
RT12
]
.

Lemma 3.23
.

There exists
t\leq r
such that:

\E_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{t}\sim[M]}\E_{i,j\sim[M]}[I(Y_{i};Y_{j}\mid Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{t}})]\leq\frac{\log q}{r-1},

where
q
is the size of the domain of
Y_{i}
.

Note that the above lemma holds as long as there is a local collection of distributions over the variables
(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{M})
that are valid probability distributions over all collections of
t+2
variables and are consistent with each other. We will apply the above lemma to reduce the global correlation between
(Y_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y_{u_{2},v_{2}})
(similarly for
Y^{\prime}
), for the local collection of distributions
\mathcal{L}
(Definition
3.5
).

Lemma 3.24

(Lemma
3.19
restated)
.

For all
\tau,p,\beta,\nu\in(0,1)
and
|\Sigma|,D,D^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
D^{\prime}\geq\max(D,\widetilde{\Omega}(1/\nu))
the following holds: Suppose there is a degree
D^{\prime}+\widetilde{\Omega}(\frac{\log|\Sigma|}{p\nu\tau})
pseudodistribution
\mu
over UG assignments that satisfies
\mathcal{A}_{I}
, and a polynomial
E(X,X^{\prime})
satisfying
\pE_{(X,X^{\prime})\sim\mu\times\mu}[E(X,X^{\prime})]\geq p
and
\mathcal{A}_{I}\vdash_{D}E(X,X^{\prime})\leq 1
. Then for all subsets
S\subseteq[N]
, there exist subsets
A,B\subseteq S
of size at most
O(\frac{\log|\Sigma|}{p\tau})
and strings
y_{A},y_{B}
such that conditioning
\mu
on the events
Y_{A}=y_{A}
and
Y_{B}=y_{B}
gives pseudodistributions
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
of degree at least
D^{\prime}
such that:

1.

\pE_{X,X^{\prime}\sim\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[E(X,X^{\prime})]\geq\frac{p}{2}
.

2.

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},v_{1}\sim S:u_{1}\neq v_{1}\\
u_{2},v_{2}\sim S:u_{2}\neq v_{2}\end{subarray}}[I(Y_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y_{u_{2},v_{2}})]\leq\tau.

3.

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},v_{1}\sim S:u_{1}\neq v_{1}\\
u_{2},v_{2}\sim S:u_{2}\neq v_{2}\end{subarray}}[I(Y^{\prime}_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y^{\prime}_{u_{2},v_{2}})]\leq\tau
,

where for
A\subseteq[N]
,
Y_{A}=(X_{i_{1}},\ldots,X_{i_{|A|}},p_{i_{1}},\ldots,p_{i_{|A|}})
(same for
Y^{\prime}
) and the mutual information is with respect to the collection of local distributions
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu})
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality suppose that
S\subseteq[N]
is
[m]
. Let
{[m]\choose r}
denote the set of
r
-sized subsets of
[m]
and
m_{r}
denote
{m\choose r}
. Let
Y=(Y_{a_{1}},\ldots,Y_{a_{m_{2}}})
for
a_{t}=(u,v)\in{[m]\choose 2}
. For brevity we will use
[m_{2}]
for
{[m]\choose 2}
. Given the pseudodistribution
\mu\times\mu
over assignments
(X,X^{\prime})
we will consider the
local collection of distributions
\mathcal{L}(\mu\times\mu,p_{\beta,\nu})
over the variables of
X,X^{\prime},p,p^{\prime}
and let this also denote the induced collection of distributions over
(Y,Y^{\prime})
(Definition
3.5
). Recall the notation for random variable
Y_{A}
, for any
A\subseteq[m]
:
Y_{A}=(X_{i_{1}},\ldots,X_{i_{|A|}},p_{i_{1}},\ldots,p_{i_{|A|}})
. We will use
y_{S}
to denote instantiations of
Y_{S}
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
denote
\mathcal{L}(\mu\times\mu,p_{\beta,\nu})
.
Applying Lemma
3.23
to the collection of distributions
\mathcal{L}
over
Y
we get that for all
\tau^{\prime}>0
, conditioning on
t=O(\frac{\log|\Sigma|}{\tau^{\prime}})
variables gives:

\E_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{t}\sim[m_{2}]}[\E_{a,b\sim[m_{2}]}I(Y_{a};Y_{b}\mid Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{t}})]\leq\tau^{\prime}.

(14)

Let
\mathcal{L}|_{R}
denote the distribution that
\mathcal{L}
induces on the random variable
R
. We know that the distribution
\mathcal{L}|_{(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{t}})}
can be reduced to the distribution
\mathcal{L}|_{Y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}}}
by discarding repeating indices in
i_{1},\ldots,i_{t}
. This is because
\mathcal{L}
is consistent on the value it assigns to a vertex
u
when it occurs in
Y_{u,v}
or
Y_{u,v^{\prime}}
. We will drop the subscript
R
from
\mathcal{L}|_{R}
when the random variable is clear from context. Expanding the definition of conditional mutual information we therefore get:

\displaystyle I(Y_{a};Y_{b}\mid Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{t}})

\displaystyle=\E_{(y_{i_{1}},\ldots,y_{i_{t}})\sim\mathcal{L}|_{(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{t}})}}[I(Y_{a};Y_{b}\mid Y_{i_{1}}=y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{t}}=y_{i_{t}})]

\displaystyle=\E_{y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}}\sim\mathcal{L}|_{Y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}}}}[I(Y_{a};Y_{b}\mid Y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}}=y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}}].

Plugging the above into equation (
14
) and applying Markov’s inequality we get that for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
:

\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\ldots,i_{t}\sim[m_{2}]\\
y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}}\sim\mathcal{L}\end{subarray}}\left[\E_{a,b\sim[m_{2}]}[I(Y_{a};Y_{b}\mid Y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}}=y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}})]\geq\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\alpha}\right]\leq\alpha.

(15)

Repeating the above analysis for
Y^{\prime}
we get:

\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1},\ldots,j_{t}\sim[m_{2}]\\
y^{\prime}_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t}}\sim\mathcal{L}\end{subarray}}\left[\E_{a,b\sim[m_{2}]}[I(Y_{a};Y_{b}\mid Y^{\prime}_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t}}=y_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t}})]\geq\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\alpha}\right]\leq\alpha.

(16)

Now recall that (Definition
3.5
) the probability of the event
(X_{u},p_{u})=(\sigma,b)
under
\mathcal{L}
, for some
\sigma\in\Sigma
and
b\in\{0,1\}
, corresponds to the pseudoexpectation under
\mu
of an appropriate polynomial we will denote by
Q_{u,(\sigma,b)}
:

\displaystyle\pPr_{\mathcal{L}}[(X_{u},p_{u})=(\sigma,b)]

\displaystyle=\pE[X_{u,\sigma}(1-b+(-1)^{1-b}p(\mathrm{val}_{u}(X)))]

\displaystyle:=\pE_{\mu}[Q_{u,(\sigma,b)}].

(17)

We can extend the above definition by letting
Q_{S,\cup_{u\in S}(\sigma_{u},b_{u})}
denote the polynomial
\prod_{u\in S}Q_{u,(\sigma_{u},b_{u})}
. For
y_{S}=(\cup_{u\in S}\sigma_{u},\cup_{u\in S}b_{u})
we get that:

\displaystyle\pPr_{\mathcal{L}}[(\cup_{u\in S}X_{u},\cup_{u\in S}p_{u})=Y_{S}=y_{S}]=\pE_{\mu}[Q_{S,\cup_{u\in S}(\sigma_{u},b_{u})}].

(18)

One can define analogous notation for
Y^{\prime}
(e.g. the polynomials
Q^{\prime}_{u,(\sigma,b)}
, etc) and derive the statements above. We know that
\mathcal{A}_{I}\vdash_{t~\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)}Q_{S,\cup_{u\in S}(\sigma_{u},b_{u})}\in[0,1]
. For ease of notation let us define the following expression:

\displaystyle\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[E(X,X^{\prime})\mid Y_{S}=y_{S},Y^{\prime}_{T}=y^{\prime}_{T}]

\displaystyle:=\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[E(X,X^{\prime})\mid Q_{S,y_{S}}\cdot Q^{\prime}_{T,y^{\prime}_{T}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[E(X,X^{\prime})Q_{S,y_{S}}Q^{\prime}_{T,y^{\prime}_{T}}]}{\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[Q_{S,y_{S}}Q^{\prime}_{T,y^{\prime}_{T}}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[E(X,X^{\prime})Q_{S,y_{S}}Q^{\prime}_{T,y^{\prime}_{T}}]}{\pPr_{\mathcal{L}}[Y_{S}=y_{S},Y^{\prime}_{T}=y^{\prime}_{T}]},

where the last equality follows from equation (
18
). Analogous to the definition of conditional expectation we can check that:

\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[E(X,X^{\prime})]=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\ldots,i_{t}\sim[m]\\
j_{1},\ldots,j_{t}\sim[m]\\
y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}},y^{\prime}_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t}}\sim\mathcal{L}\end{subarray}}[\pE_{X,X^{\prime}}[E(X,X^{\prime})\mid Y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}}=y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}},Y^{\prime}_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t}}=y^{\prime}_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t}}]].

Since
\mathcal{A}_{I}\vdash_{D}E(X,X^{\prime})\leq 1
and
\deg(\mu)\geq D+\widetilde{\Omega}(t/\nu)
we get that
E(X,X^{\prime})\leq 1
even after conditioning on a non-negative event
Q
of degree
\widetilde{O}(t/\nu)
:
\pE[E(X,X^{\prime})\mid Q]\leq 1
. An averaging argument implies that:

\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\ldots,j_{1},\ldots\sim[m_{2}]\\
y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}},y^{\prime}_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t}}\sim\mathcal{L}\end{subarray}}\left[\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[E(X,X^{\prime})\mid Y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}}=y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}},Y^{\prime}_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t}}=y^{\prime}_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t}}]\geq\frac{p}{2}\right]\geq\frac{p}{2}.

(19)

Choosing
\alpha=p/8
and
\tau^{\prime}<p\tau/8
we can take a union bound over the events in equations (
15
), (
16
), (
19
) to get that there exist sets
i_{1},\ldots,i_{t},j_{1},\ldots,j_{t}\in[m_{2}]
and
y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}},y^{\prime}_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t}}\sim\mathcal{L}
such that,

\E_{a,b\sim[m_{2}]}[I(Y_{a};Y_{b}\mid Y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}}=y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}}]\leq\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\alpha}<\tau.

\E_{a,b\sim[m_{2}]}[I(Y^{\prime}_{a};Y^{\prime}_{b}\mid Y^{\prime}_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t}}=y^{\prime}_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t}})]\leq\frac{\tau^{\prime}}{\alpha}<\tau.

\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[E(X,X^{\prime})\mid Y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}}=y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}},Y^{\prime}_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t}}=y^{\prime}_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t}}]\geq\frac{p}{2}.

Let
\mu_{1}
be the pseudodistribution on
X
that we get by conditioning
\mu
on
Q_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t},y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}}}
and let
\mu_{2}
be the pseudodistribution on
X^{\prime}
that we get by conditioning
\mu
on
Q_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t},y^{\prime}_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t}}}
. It is easy to check that:

\mathcal{L}(\mu\times\mu,p_{\beta,\nu})|(Y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}}=y_{i_{1}\cup\ldots\cup i_{t}},Y^{\prime}_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t}}=y^{\prime}_{j_{1}\cup\ldots\cup j_{t}})=\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu}),

thus giving us the three properties we need in the lemma. It remains to check the degree bounds on
\mu
that we require. We have conditioned on
t=O(\log|\Sigma|/p\tau)
variables from
(Y,Y^{\prime})
, each having degree
\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)
in
X,X^{\prime}
, therefore it suffices to have degree of
\mu
to be
\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{\log|\Sigma|}{p\tau\nu}\right)
. After conditioning we get that
\deg(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2})\geq D^{\prime}
if
\deg(\mu)\geq D^{\prime}+\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{\log|\Sigma|}{p\tau\nu}\right)
thus completing the proof.
∎

3.5
Proof of Lemma
3.10
:
Conditioning Does Not Introduce Correlations

In this section we prove that given a product distribution over
(X,X^{\prime})
that satisfies low global correlation with respect to the variables
(Y,Y^{\prime})
, where
Y_{u,v}=(X_{u},X_{v},p_{u},p_{v})
, and an event
E(X,X^{\prime})
that holds with large probability, conditioning on
E
cannot correlate too many pairs
(Y_{i},Y^{\prime}_{i})
. This is a general lemma that holds if the variables
(Y,Y^{\prime})
are local functions of the underlying variables
(X,X^{\prime})
and satisfy low global correlation, but here we state it for our specific application only.

Lemma 3.25

(Restatement of Lemma
3.10
)
.

For all
\tau,p,\beta,\nu,\delta\in(0,1)
, integers
D,N
,
m\leq N
the following holds: Let
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
be a degree
D+\widetilde{\Omega}(1/\nu)
pseudodistribution over
(X,X^{\prime})
satisfying
\mathcal{A}_{I}(X,X^{\prime})
and
E(X,X^{\prime})
be a polynomial such that,
\mathcal{A}_{I}\vdash_{D}E(X,X^{\prime})\in[0,1]
and
\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[E(X,X^{\prime})]\geq p
. Suppose for
S\subseteq[N]
we have that,

1.

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},v_{1}\sim S:u_{1}\neq v_{1}\\
u_{2},v_{2}\sim S:u_{2}\neq v_{2}\end{subarray}}[I(Y_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y_{u_{2},v_{2}})]\leq\tau.

2.

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},v_{1}\sim S:u_{1}\neq v_{1}\\
u_{2},v_{2}\sim S:u_{2}\neq v_{2}\end{subarray}}[I(Y^{\prime}_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y^{\prime}_{u_{2},v_{2}})]\leq\tau
,

where
Y_{u,v}=(X_{u},X_{v},p_{u},p_{v}),Y^{\prime}_{u,v}=(X^{\prime}_{u},X^{\prime}_{v},p^{\prime}_{u},p^{\prime}_{v})
and the mutual information is with respect to the collection of local distributions
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu})
(Definition
3.5
). Then we have that,

\Pr_{u,v\sim S:u\neq v}[TV((Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})|E,(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v}))\geq\delta]\leq O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\tau}+1/|S|}{p\delta^{2}}\right),

where the distribution
(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})|E
refers to the joint distribution on these variables defined by the collection of local distributions
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|E,p_{\beta,\nu})
and similarly
(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})
refers to the distribution defined by
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu})
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
3.19
, without loss of generality suppose
S=[m]
, let
[m_{2}]
denote
2
-sized subsets of
m
and
Y=(Y_{a_{1}},\ldots,Y_{a_{M}})
for
a_{t}=(u,v),(u,v)\in[m_{2}]
. Given the pseudodistribution
\mathcal{D}
over assignments
(X,X^{\prime})
we will consider the local collection of distributions
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D},p_{\beta,\nu})
over the variables
X,X^{\prime},p,p^{\prime}
and therefore the induced collection of distributions over
(Y,Y^{\prime})=(Y_{a_{1}},\ldots,Y_{a_{M}},Y^{\prime}_{a_{1}},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{a_{M}})
(Definition
3.5
).

Throughout the proof we will consider random variables
(Y,Y^{\prime})
drawn from the collection of local distributions
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu})
and from the conditioned collection of distributions
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|E,p_{\beta,\nu})
. For brevity of notation we will use
(Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})|E
to be the joint distribution induced by
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|E,p_{\beta,\nu})
on
(Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})
and use
(Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})
to be the distribution induced by
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu})
. We will abbreviate
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu})
to
\mathcal{L}
. Let
U
be the set of variables
a\in[m_{2}]
for which
TV((Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})|E,(Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a}))\geq\delta
and let the fractional-size of
U
be
\gamma
. If
\Omega
denotes the domain of
Y_{u},Y^{\prime}_{u}
then for every
a\in U
there exists a set
S_{a}\subseteq\Omega\times\Omega
such that:

\pPr_{\mathcal{L}}[(Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})\in S_{a}\mid E]-\pPr_{\mathcal{L}}[(Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})\in S_{a}]\geq\delta.

(20)

Let
e_{a}
denote
\pPr_{\mathcal{L}}[(Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})\in S_{a}]
. Define the random variables
Z_{a}=\mathbb{1}((Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})\in S_{a})-e_{a}
. Define:

Z=\E_{a\sim U}[Z_{a}]=\E_{a\in U}[\mathbb{1}((Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})\in S_{a})-e_{a}].

Let
\pE_{\mathcal{L}}
denote the natural expectation operator corresponding to the local distributions
\mathcal{L}
.
One can check that
\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[Z]=\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[Z_{a}]=0
, and we now calculate its variance. For two events
A,B
on the variables
Y,Y^{\prime}
let
\widetilde{\Cov}(A,B)
denote
\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[AB]-\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[A]\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[B]
. Firstly for all
a,b\in[m_{2}]
using Pinsker’s inequality and data processing inequality we have that,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Cov}(\mathbb{1}((Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})\in S_{a}),\mathbb{1}((Y_{b},Y^{\prime}_{b})\in S_{b}))

\displaystyle\leq TV((Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a},Y_{b},Y^{\prime}_{b}),(Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})\times(Y_{b},Y^{\prime}_{b}))

\displaystyle\leq O(\sqrt{I(Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a};Y_{b},Y^{\prime}_{b})})

\displaystyle\leq O(\sqrt{I(Y_{a};Y_{b})+I(Y^{\prime}_{a};Y^{\prime}_{b})})

The proof will proceed by proving upper and lower bounds on
\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[Z^{2}]
, where the upper bound uses low global correlation properties of
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
and the lower bound uses the large deviation we have by equation
20
.

Upper bound for
\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[Z^{2}]
:

We have the following upper bound:

\displaystyle\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[Z^{2}]

\displaystyle=\E_{a,b\sim U}[\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[(\mathbb{1}((Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})\in S_{a})-e_{a})(\mathbb{1}((Y_{b},Y^{\prime}_{b})\in S_{b})-e_{b})]]

\displaystyle=\E_{a,b\sim U}[\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[\mathbb{1}((Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})\in S_{a})-e_{a}]\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[\mathbb{1}((Y_{b},Y^{\prime}_{b})\in S_{b})-e_{b}]+\widetilde{\Cov}(\mathbb{1}((Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})\in S_{a}),\mathbb{1}((Y_{b},Y^{\prime}_{b})\in S_{b}))]

\displaystyle\leq\E_{a,b\sim U}[O(\sqrt{I(Y_{a};Y_{b})+I(Y^{\prime}_{a};Y^{\prime}_{b})})]

\displaystyle\leq O(\sqrt{\E_{a,b\sim U}[I(Y_{a};Y_{b})+I(Y^{\prime}_{a};Y^{\prime}_{b})}])

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{\gamma}\right),

where the last inequality follows because
\E_{a,b\sim[m_{2}]}[I(Y_{a};Y_{b})]\geq\gamma^{2}\E_{a,b\sim U}[I(Y_{a};Y_{b})]
, and by assumption
\E_{a,b\sim[M]}[I(Y_{a};Y_{b})]\leq\tau
(similarly for
Y^{\prime}
).

Lower bound for
\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[Z^{2}]
:

Let
Q_{a}
be the polynomial corresponding to the event
\Ind[(Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})\in S_{a}]
, i.e.
\pPr_{\mathcal{L}}[(Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})\in S_{a}]=\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[Q_{a}]
. Let
P(X,X^{\prime})
abbreviated as
P
denote the polynomial
\E_{a}[Q_{a}-e_{a}]
. Let
Q_{a\cup b}
be the polynomial such that
\pPr_{\mathcal{L}}[(Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})\in S_{a}\wedge(Y_{b},Y^{\prime}_{b})\in S_{b}]=\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[Q_{a\cup b}]
. If
a\cap b=\phi
we have that
Q_{a\cup b}=Q_{a}\cdot Q_{b}
, whereas this may not hold if they intersect in one or two variables. But we have that
\Pr_{a,b\sim U}[a\cap b\neq\phi]\leq O(1/|U|)
.
Using these facts we first show that,
\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[Z^{2}]+O(1/|U|)\geq\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P^{2}]
via the following two equations:

\displaystyle\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[Z^{2}]

\displaystyle=\E_{a,b\sim U}[\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[(\mathbb{1}((Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})\in S_{a})-e_{a})(\mathbb{1}((Y_{b},Y^{\prime}_{b})\in S_{b})-e_{b})]]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{a,b\sim U}[a\cap b=\phi]\E_{a,b\sim U:a\cap b=\phi}[\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[(Q_{a}-e_{a})(Q_{b}-e_{b})]]

\displaystyle+\Pr_{a,b\sim U}[a\cap b\neq\phi]\E_{a,b\sim U:a\cap b\neq\phi}[\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[(\mathbb{1}((Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})\in S_{a})-e_{a})(\mathbb{1}((Y_{b},Y^{\prime}_{b})\in S_{b})-e_{b})]]

\displaystyle\geq\Pr_{a,b\sim U}[a\cap b=\phi]\E_{a,b\sim U:a\cap b=\phi}[\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[(Q_{a}-e_{a})(Q_{b}-e_{b})]]-O\left(\frac{1}{|U|}\right).

(21)

On the other hand we have that:

\displaystyle\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P^{2}]

\displaystyle=\E_{a,b\sim U}[\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[(Q_{a}-e_{a})(Q_{b}-e_{b})]]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{a,b\sim U}[a\cap b=\phi]\E_{a,b\sim U:a\cap b=\phi}[\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[(Q_{a}-e_{a})(Q_{b}-e_{b})]]

\displaystyle+\Pr_{a,b\sim U}[a\cap b\neq\phi]\E_{a,b\sim U:a\cap b=\phi}[\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[(Q_{a}-e_{a})(Q_{b}-e_{b})]]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr_{a,b\sim U}[a\cap b=\phi]\E_{a,b\sim U:a\cap b\neq\phi}[\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[(Q_{a}-e_{a})(Q_{b}-e_{b})]]+O\left(\frac{1}{|U|}\right)

(22)

Combining (
21
) and (
22
) we get that:

\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[Z^{2}]+O\left(\frac{1}{|U|}\right)\geq\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P^{2}].

Recall that
|U|=\gamma|S|
. Since
\mathcal{A}_{I}\vdash_{D}E(X,X^{\prime})\in[0,1]
we get that:

\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[Z^{2}]+O\left(\frac{1}{\gamma|S|}\right)\geq\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P^{2}]\geq\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[E]\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P^{2}|E]\geq p\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P|E]^{2}=p\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[Z|E]^{2},

where the last inequality is by Cauchy-Schwarz on the pseudodistribution
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|E
and the last equality follows by the definition of
P
and
Z
. Using equation (
20
) we know that for all
a\in U
:

\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[Z_{a}\mid E]=\pPr_{\mathcal{L}}[(Y_{a},Y^{\prime}_{a})\in S_{a}\mid E]-e_{a}\geq\delta,

which implies that
\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[Z\mid E]\geq\delta
.

Combining the upper and lower bounds on
\pE_{\mathcal{L}}[Z^{2}]
we get that
\gamma\leq O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\tau}+1/|S|}{p\delta^{2}}\right)
,
completing the proof of the lemma.

∎

3.6
Proof of Lemma
3.11
: Rounding Subgraphs with Large Shift Potential

In this section, we will show that when the shift-partition potential
\Phi^{G}_{\beta,\nu}
(Definition
3.3
) is large with respect to a pseudodistribution
\mathcal{D}
(with certain nice properties), then the Condition&Round Algorithm (Algorithm
3.7
) succeeds in returning a good assignment for the unique games instance. The proofs in this section follow along the lines of the analysis of Condition&Round given in
[
BBK+21
]
, albeit instead of independence between
X,X^{\prime}
we only have approximate local independence. Since we will always be working with the graph
G
we will henceforth drop the superscript
G
from the shift-partition potential
\Phi
.
We will prove the following theorem in this section:

Lemma 3.26

(Restatement of Lemma
3.11
)
.

Let
I=(G,\Pi)
be an affine instance of Unique Games over the alphabet
\Sigma
and
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
be a degree
D+\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)
pseudodistribution over assignments
(X,X^{\prime})
to
I
. Let
E(X,X^{\prime})
be a polynomial such that
\mathcal{A}_{I}(X,X^{\prime})\vdash_{D}E(X,X^{\prime})\in[0,1]
. Suppose we have that:

\Pr_{u,v\sim V(G):u\neq v}[TV((Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})|E,(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v}))>\delta]\leq\zeta,

where the distribution
(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})|E
refers to the joint distribution on these variables defined by the collection of local distributions
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|E,p_{\beta,\nu})
and similarly
(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})
refers to the distribution defined by
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu})
(Definition
3.5
).

If
\Phi^{G}_{\beta,\nu}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|E(X,X^{\prime}))\geq\gamma
, then on at least one of the pseudodistributions
\mu^{\text{sym}}_{1}
or
\mu^{\text{sym}}_{2}
Algorithm
3.7
runs in time
\mathrm{poly}(|V(G)|)
and returns an assignment of expected value at least

(\beta-\nu)^{2}\left(\gamma-O(\delta+\zeta)-\frac{1}{|V(G)|}\right)-3\nu(\beta-\nu)

for
I
.

Proof of Lemma
3.26
.

Following the proof strategy of
[
BBK+21
]
we define the alternate shift potential
\Psi(\mu)
:

Definition 3.27

(Alternate shift potential
[
BBK+21
]
)
.

The alternate shift potential of a degree-
D\geq 4
pseudodistribution
\mu
is given by

\Psi(\mu):=\E_{u,v\sim\pi(G)}\left[\sum_{s\in\Sigma}\pPr_{\mu}[X_{v}-X_{u}=s]^{2}\cdot\pE[\mathrm{val}_{v}(X)\mid X_{v}-X_{u}=s]\right],

where
\pi(G)
is the stationary measure on
G
and
\mathrm{val}_{v}(X)
denotes the value of vertex
v
.

We will show that if
\Phi(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|E)
is large and
E
does not introduce too many correlations, then either
\Psi(\mu_{1})
or
\Psi(\mu_{2})
must be large:

Lemma 3.28
.

Let
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
be a degree
\widetilde{O}(D+1/\nu)
pseudodistribution over
(X,X^{\prime})
and let
E(X,X^{\prime})
be a polynomial such that
\mathcal{A}_{I}\vdash_{D}E(X,X^{\prime})\in[0,1]
. Suppose we have that:

\Pr_{u,v\sim V(G):u\neq v}[TV((Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})|E,(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v}))>\delta]\leq\zeta.

If the shift-partition potential of
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|E(X,X^{\prime})
is large, then the alternate shift-potential of
\mu_{1}
or
\mu_{2}
must be large as well:

\Phi_{\beta,\nu}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|E(X,X^{\prime}))\leq\frac{\Psi(\mu_{1})}{2(\beta-\nu)^{2}}+\frac{\Psi(\mu_{2})}{2(\beta-\nu)^{2}}+\frac{3\nu}{\beta-\nu}+2\delta+2\zeta+\frac{1}{|V(G)|}.

Proof.

Deferred to Section
3.6.1
.
∎

Given a pseudodistribution
\mu
, we
recall that
\mu^{sym}
is the shift symmetrized pseudodistribution as in Section
2.4.3
.
We note that
\Psi(\mu)=\Psi(\mu^{\text{sym}})
because
\Psi
as
\pPr_{\mu^{\text{sym}}}[X_{u}-X_{v}=s]=\pPr_{\mu}[X_{u}-X_{v}=s]
and
\pE_{\mu^{\text{sym}}}[\mathrm{val}_{v}(X)|X_{v}-X_{u}=s]=\pE_{\mu}[\mathrm{val}_{v}(X)|X_{v}-X_{u}=s]
for all
s
. Therefore we can use the following lemma from
[
BBK+21
]
that shows that when the alternate shift potential of a shift-symmetric pseudodistribution is large, a single step of conditioning and rounding returns a solution of high objective value:

Lemma 3.29

(Lemma 3.6
[
BBK+21
]
)
.

Let
I=(G,\Pi)
be an affine instance of Unique Games over the alphabet
\Sigma
, let
\kappa>0
and
let
\mu
be a degree-
4
shift-symmetric pseudodistribution for
I
. If
\Psi(\mu)\geq\kappa
, then Algorithm
3.7
returns a solution of expected value at least
\kappa
.

The statement now follows by applying the last lemma on either
\mu^{\text{sym}}_{1}
or
\mu^{\text{sym}}_{2}
(depending
which one has a higher
\Psi
).
∎

3.6.1
Relating the potentials: Proof of Lemma
3.28

Proof of Lemma
3.28
.

We begin by recalling that in the definition of
\Phi_{\beta,\eta}
, we used an
\nu
-additive polynomial approximation
p(x)
of degree
\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)
to the indicator function
\Ind[x\geq\beta]
on the interval
x\in[0,1]
, guaranteed by Theorem
2.7
. We will use
E
to denote the polynomial
E(X,X^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{D}
to denote the pseudodistribution
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|E
and
\pi
to denote the uniform (in general stationary) measure over
V(G)
. Let
p_{u}
denote the polynomial
p_{\beta,\nu}(\mathrm{val}_{u}(X))
and
p^{\prime}_{u}=p_{\beta,\nu}(\mathrm{val}_{u}(X^{\prime}))
. Recall also the collection of local distributions
\mathcal{L}(\cdot,p_{\beta,\nu})
(Definition
3.5
). We will overload the notation
p_{u},p^{\prime}_{u}
to mean the variables from Definition
3.5
and the polynomial
p_{\beta,\nu}(\mathrm{val}_{u}(X))
, and the use should be clear from context. Expanding the definition of
\Phi_{\beta,\nu}(\mathcal{D})
we get:

\displaystyle\Phi_{\beta,\nu}(\mathcal{D})

\displaystyle=\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[\sum_{s\in\Sigma}\left(\E_{u\sim\pi}\Ind[X_{u}-X_{u}^{\prime}=s]p_{u}p^{\prime}_{u}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[\sum_{s\in\Sigma}\E_{u,v\sim\pi}\Ind[X_{u}-X_{u}^{\prime}=X_{v}-X_{v}^{\prime}=s]p_{u}p_{v}p^{\prime}_{u}p^{\prime}_{v}\right]

\displaystyle=\E_{u,v\sim\pi}\pE_{\mathcal{D}}\left[\Ind[X_{u}-X_{v}=X^{\prime}_{u}-X^{\prime}_{v}]\cdot p_{u}p_{v}p^{\prime}_{u}p^{\prime}_{v}\right],

\displaystyle=\E_{u,v\sim\pi}[\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}\left[\Ind[X_{u}-X_{v}=X^{\prime}_{u}-X^{\prime}_{v}]\cdot p_{u}p_{v}p^{\prime}_{u}p^{\prime}_{v}\right]+{\sf err}(u,v)],

(23)

for
{\sf err}(u,v)
defined accordingly for each pair
(u,v)
(note that we have switched from the pseudo-distribution
\mathcal{D}
to the pseudo-distribution
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
in the last transition).

Bounding the error term in (
23
).

For
u=v
, we get that
{\sf err}(u,v)=\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[p_{u}^{2}(p^{\prime}_{u})^{2}]-\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[p_{u}^{2}(p^{\prime}_{u})^{2}]\leq 1
. Let us bound
{\sf err}(u,v)
for
u\neq v
.
Let
R(X_{u},X_{v},X^{\prime}_{u},X^{\prime}_{v},p_{u},p_{v},p^{\prime}_{u},p^{\prime}_{v})
be the polynomial
\Ind[X_{u}-X_{v}=X^{\prime}_{u}-X^{\prime}_{v}]p_{u}p_{v}p^{\prime}_{u}p^{\prime}_{v}
(
p_{u}
denotes the polynomial
p_{\beta,\nu}(\mathrm{val}_{u}(X))
). Here we will overload the notation of
p_{u}
to also denote the indicator variables from Definition
3.5
and let
R(a_{1},\ldots,a_{4},b_{1},\ldots,b_{4})
for
a_{1},\ldots,a_{4}\in\Sigma
and
b_{1},\ldots,b_{4}\in\{0,1\}
also denote the evaluation of
R(X_{u},\ldots,p^{\prime}_{v})
on the values
a_{1},\ldots,b_{4}
:
\Ind[a_{1}-a_{2}=a_{3}-a_{4}]b_{1}b_{2}b_{3}b_{4}
. Using Definition
3.5
we have that when
u\neq v
:
\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[R]=\pPr_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D},p_{\beta,\nu})}[X_{u}-X_{v}=X^{\prime}_{u}-X^{\prime}_{v},p_{u}=p_{v}=p^{\prime}_{u}=p^{\prime}_{v}=1]
and similarly for the distribution
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
in place of
\mathcal{D}
. Therefore we get that for
u\neq v
:

\displaystyle{\sf err}(u,v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pE_{\mathcal{D}}[R(X_{u},\ldots,p^{\prime}_{v})]-\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[R(X_{u},\ldots,p^{\prime}_{v})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pPr_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D},p_{\beta,\nu})}[X_{u}-X_{v}=X^{\prime}_{u}-X^{\prime}_{v},p_{u}=\ldots=p^{\prime}_{v}=1]-\pPr_{\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu})}[X_{u}-X_{v}=X^{\prime}_{u}-X^{\prime}_{v},p_{u}=\ldots=p^{\prime}_{v}=1]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1},\ldots,a_{4}\in\Sigma\end{subarray}}R(a_{1},\ldots,a_{4},1,\ldots,1)(\pPr_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D},p_{\beta,\nu})}[X_{u}=a_{1},\ldots,p_{u}=1,\ldots]-\pPr_{\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu})}[X_{u}=a_{1},\ldots,p_{u}=1\ldots])

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1},\ldots,a_{4}\in\Sigma\end{subarray}}\left|\pPr_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{D},p_{\beta,\nu})}[X_{u}=a_{1},\ldots,p_{u}=1,\ldots]-\pPr_{\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu})}[X_{u}=a_{1},\ldots,p_{u}=1,\ldots]\right|

\displaystyle=2TV((Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})|E,(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})),

where
Y_{u,v}=(X_{u},X_{v},p_{u},p_{v})
,
Y^{\prime}_{u,v}=(X^{\prime}_{u},X^{\prime}_{v},p^{\prime}_{u},p^{\prime}_{v})
as in the lemma, the distribution
(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})|E
refers to the joint distribution on these variables defined by the collection of local distributions
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|E,p_{\beta,\nu})
and similarly
(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})
refers to the distribution defined by
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu})
(Definition
3.5
).

Combining, we get that:

\displaystyle\E_{u,v\sim\pi}[{\sf err}(u,v)]

\displaystyle\leq\E_{u,v\sim\pi:u\neq v}[{\sf err}(u,v)]+\Pr_{u,v\sim\pi}[u=v]\E_{u}[{\sf err}(u,u)]

\displaystyle\leq\E_{u,v\sim\pi:u\neq v}[2TV((Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v})|E,(Y_{u,v},Y^{\prime}_{u,v}))]+\frac{1}{|V(G)|}

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta+2\zeta+\frac{1}{|V(G)|},

(24)

by the assumption in the lemma statement.

Bounding the main term in (
23
).

We now upper bound the first term in (
23
).

\displaystyle\E_{u,v\sim\pi}[\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}\left[\Ind[X_{u}-X_{v}=X^{\prime}_{u}-X^{\prime}_{v}]\cdot p_{u}p_{v}p^{\prime}_{u}p^{\prime}_{v}\right]

\displaystyle=\E_{u,v\sim\pi}\left[\sum_{s\in\Sigma}\pE_{\mu_{1}}[\Ind[X_{u}-X_{v}=s]\cdot p_{u}p_{v}]\pE_{\mu_{2}}[\Ind[X^{\prime}_{u}-X^{\prime}_{v}=s]\cdot p^{\prime}_{u}p^{\prime}_{v}]\right].

We next use that
p(x),\mathrm{val}_{u}(X),\mathrm{val}_{u}(X^{\prime})\in[0,1]
and Fact
2.8
asserting that

p(x)\leq\frac{x}{\beta-\nu}+\nu
for all
x\in[0,1]
, and furthermore this is SoS-certifiable.
Thus, pulling out a factor of
p_{v},p^{\prime}_{v}
and applying
Fact
2.8
to bound
p_{u},p^{\prime}_{u}
we get
that the first term on the right hand side of (
23
) is at most

\displaystyle\E_{u,v\sim\pi}\left[\sum_{s\in\Sigma}\pE_{\mu_{1}}\left[\Ind[X_{u}-X_{v}=s]\cdot\frac{\mathrm{val}_{u}(X)}{\beta-\nu}\right]\pE_{\mu_{2}}\left[\Ind[X^{\prime}_{u}-X^{\prime}_{v}=s]\cdot\frac{\mathrm{val}_{u}(X^{\prime})}{\beta-\nu}\right]\right]+\frac{3\nu}{\beta-\nu}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\E_{u,v\sim\pi}\left[\sum_{s\in\Sigma}\pE_{\mu_{1}}\left[\Ind[X_{u}-X_{v}=s]\cdot\frac{\mathrm{val}_{u}(X)}{\beta-\nu}\right]^{2}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\E_{u,v\sim\pi}\left[\sum_{s\in\Sigma}\pE_{\mu_{2}}\left[\Ind[X^{\prime}_{u}-X^{\prime}_{v}=s]\cdot\frac{\mathrm{val}_{u}(X^{\prime})}{\beta-\nu}\right]^{2}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{3\nu}{\beta-\nu},

where we have used that the AM-GM inequality. The first term may be bounded as

\displaystyle\E_{u,v\sim\pi}\left[\sum_{s\in\Sigma}\pE_{\mu_{1}}\left[\Ind[X_{u}-X_{v}=s]\cdot\frac{\mathrm{val}_{u}(X)}{\beta-\nu}\right]^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\E_{u,v\sim\pi}\left[\sum_{s\in\Sigma}\pE_{\mu_{1}}\left[\Ind[X_{u}-X_{v}=s]\cdot\frac{1}{\beta-\nu}\right]\pE_{\mu_{1}}\left[\Ind[X_{u}-X_{v}=s]\frac{\mathrm{val}_{u}(X)}{\beta-\nu}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\beta-\nu)^{2}}\E_{u,v\sim\pi}\left[\sum_{s\in\Sigma}\pE_{\mu_{1}}[\Ind[X_{u}-X_{v}=s]]^{2}\cdot\pE_{\mu_{1}}[\mathrm{val}_{u}(X)\mid X_{u}-X_{v}=s]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Psi(\mu_{1})}{(\beta-\nu)^{2}},

where we have used that
\mathrm{val}_{u}(X)\leq 1
and applied the definition of conditional pseudoexpectation,
and similarly the second term is upper bounded by
\frac{\Psi(\mu_{2})}{(\beta-\nu)^{2}}
. Plugging this and (
3.6.1
) into (
23
) finishes the proof.
∎

4
Unique Games with low completeness

In this section we give an analysis which works for UG instances with arbitrary small completeness (but bounded away from
0
). The only step that changes is concluding that there is a subcube with large shift-potential (after conditioning).

Recall that in the proof of the analogous lemma for large completeness (Lemma
3.9
)
we used Claims
3.21
and
3.22
to conclude that if a subcube has large global shift-partition potential it also has large shift-partition potential. This was possible
because all the vertices with high global value will also have high value inside the subcube as the expansion of the subcube is small (
O({\varepsilon})
if the completeness was
1-{\varepsilon}
). This fact is no longer true in the low completeness regime, as subcubes
now may have expansion close to
1
.
To circumvent this issue, we no longer go via the intermediate global shift-partition route and
directly try to conclude that the shift-partition potential is large on a subcube. Towards this end
we need a stronger conclusion of global hypercontractivity which we refer to
as an “edge-covering” statement: the subcubes on which some part of the shift partition is large cover nearly all of the the internal edges of the shift-partition.

As we shall see, if the value of our pseudodistribution is originally
c
, the
fraction of internal edges is at least
c^{2}
(in particular
c^{2}
-fraction of edges are satisfied by both
X
and
X^{\prime}
), and as we are able to cover almost all internal edges we get that there exists a subcube with large completeness and in fact large shift-partition potential. Given this version of Lemma
3.9
, the rest of the analysis remains the same.

Notation:

For the purposes of this section we first consider the simpler shift-partition defined by functions
\{G_{s}:V(G)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}[X,X^{\prime}]\}_{s\in\Sigma}
:

G_{s}(u)=\mathbb{1}(X_{u}-X^{\prime}_{u}=s).

Note that
G_{s}
’s cover all the vertices, that is
\sum_{s}\delta(G_{s})=1
and satisfy Booleanity:
G_{s}(u)^{2}=G_{s}(u)
for all
u\in V(G)
.

Given two assignments
X,X^{\prime}
, let
\mathrm{val}_{u}(X\wedge X^{\prime})
denote the fraction of edges incident on
u
that are satisfied by
both

X
and
X^{\prime}
. Note that
\mathrm{val}_{u}(X\wedge X^{\prime})
is a degree four polynomial in
(X,X^{\prime})
:

\mathrm{val}_{u}(X\wedge X^{\prime})=\E_{v:(u,v)\in E}[(\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}X_{u,\sigma}X_{v,\pi_{u,v}(\sigma)})(\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}X^{\prime}_{u,\sigma}X^{\prime}_{v,\pi_{u,v}(\sigma)})].

Let
\mathrm{val}_{I}(X\wedge X^{\prime})=\E_{u}[\mathrm{val}_{u}(X\wedge X^{\prime})]
denote the fraction of edges in the graph that are satisfied by both
X
and
X^{\prime}
. Further for an
i
-restriction
a\in{[n]\choose i}
and a vertex
u\in J|_{a}
let
\mathrm{val}^{a}_{u}(X\wedge X^{\prime})
denote the fraction of edges incident on
u
in
J|_{a}
that are satisfied by both
X
and
X^{\prime}
. This is also a degree
4
polynomial in
(X,X^{\prime})
.

Definition 4.1

(Approximate Indicator for Dense Subcubes)
.

Let
{\varepsilon}_{0},\ldots,{\varepsilon}_{r}\in(0,1)
to be determined later. For any
i
-restriction
a
let
R_{s,a}
denote the (approximate) indicator that
G_{s}
is
{\varepsilon}_{i}
-dense in
J|_{a}
, but not
{\varepsilon}_{|b|}
-dense inside any subcube
J|_{b}
where
b\subsetneq a
. To define this as a polynomial let us first define the approximate indicator
p_{<\beta,\nu}(x)
as
1-p_{\beta,\nu}(x)
. One can check that
p_{<\beta,\nu}
approximates
\Ind[x<\beta]
when
x\in[0,1]
with similar properties as
p_{\beta,\nu}
(Theorem
2.7
) that approximates
\Ind[x\geq\beta]
for
x\in[0,1]
. Formally using the polynomial approximation for an indicator define

R_{s,a}=p_{{\varepsilon}_{i},\nu}(\delta(G_{s}|_{a}))\prod_{j<i}\prod_{b\subset a:|b|=j}p_{<{\varepsilon}_{j},\nu}(\delta(G_{s}|_{b})).

4.1
The Edge Covering Theorem

Our argument will need an upper bound on the number of
events
R_{s,a}
that can occur simultaneously, which
roughly speaking asks how many
i
-restricted subcubes can a given set
F
be dense on.
As stated, there is no good upper bound for this:
if
F
is dense on an
i-1
-restricted subcube
then it would be quite dense on many
i
-restricted subcubes containing it. This is the reason
that in the event
R_{s,a}
, we required that the
i
-restricted subcube is dense but there is no
j
-restricted subcube (
j<i
) containing it on which our set is still somewhat dense.

Claim 4.2
.

If
{\varepsilon}_{0},\ldots,{\varepsilon}_{r}
satisfy that
{\varepsilon}_{i-1}\leq{\varepsilon}_{i}/(2^{i+1}i)
then we get that:

\vdash_{\ell^{O(\ell^{i}/\nu)}}\E_{a\sim{[n]\choose i}}[R_{s,a}]\leq\frac{4\delta(G_{s})}{{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}\ell^{i}}+O(\nu).

Proof.

We first present an argument when the polynomials
p_{\beta,\nu}
in
R_{s,a}
are replaced by indicators. We then give a sketch of how to convert the proof into an SoS proof.

Converting the
p_{\beta,\nu}
’s to indicators, we get that
R_{s,a}
corresponds to
T_{s,a}
:

\displaystyle T_{s,a}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{1}(\delta(G_{s}|_{a})\geq{\varepsilon}_{i})\prod_{j<i}\prod_{b\subset a:|b|=j}\mathbb{1}(\delta(G_{s}|_{b})<{\varepsilon}_{j})

that is the event that
G_{s}
is
{\varepsilon}_{i}
-dense in
a
, but for all subsets
b
of
a
, it is at most
{\varepsilon}_{j}
-dense. We will show that the fraction of such restrictions
a
must be small.

We consider the Fourier-analytic function
f_{i,F}
defined in Section
C
, Definition
C.6
and use its alternative formula from Lemma
C.10
:

f_{i,F}(a)=\sum_{b\subseteq a}(-1)^{i-|b|}\delta(F|_{b})\geq\delta(F|_{a})-\sum_{b\subsetneq a}\delta(F|_{b}).

Technically we have only defined these functions for the Cayley-version of the Johnson graph
C_{n,\ell,\alpha}
, but one can use the above definition for Johnson graphs and derive the same properties that we use here upto
o(1)
error terms. We will ignore these
o(1)
-error terms in this proof.

If
T_{s,a}=1
we get that,
\delta(F|_{a})\geq{\varepsilon}_{i}
, but each
\delta(F|_{b})
is at most
{\varepsilon}_{|b|}<{\varepsilon}_{i}/(2^{i+1}i)
so we get:

f_{i,G_{s}}(a)\geq{\varepsilon}_{i}-\sum_{j<i}{i\choose j}{\varepsilon}_{j}\geq{\varepsilon}_{i}-\sum_{j<i}2^{i}{\varepsilon}_{j}\geq\frac{{\varepsilon}_{i}}{2}.

This immediately gives:

T_{s,a}\leq 1\leq 4f_{i,G_{s}}(a)^{2}/{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2},

(25)

if
T_{s,a}=1
. If
T_{s,a}=0
then the above continues to hold.

On the other hand by the definition of
f_{i}
Lemma
C.11
gives that,

\E[f_{i,G_{s}}(a)^{2}]=\frac{W^{i}[G_{s}]}{\ell^{i}}\leq\frac{\E[G_{s}^{2}]}{\ell^{i}}.

Combining with equation
25
we get:

\E_{a\sim{[n]\choose i}}[T_{s,a}]\leq\E_{a}\left[\frac{4f_{i,G_{s}}(a)^{2}}{{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}}\right]\leq\frac{4\delta(G_{s})}{{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}\ell^{i}}.

SoS-ing the proof:

We sketch an SoS proof for the statement for
R_{s,a}
with
a\in{[n]\choose 1}
. The full statement follows analogously.

Let
\delta_{0}
denote
\delta(G_{s})
and
\delta_{1}
denote
\delta(G_{s}|_{a}).
Analogous to (
25
) we will first show that:

\frac{4(\delta_{1}-\delta_{0})^{2}}{{\varepsilon}_{1}^{2}}+2\nu-R_{s,a}>\nu

(26)

if
\delta_{1},\delta_{0}\in[0,1]
. This will not be an SoS proof, but after showing this we can use a blackbox theorem to convert it into an SoS proof by the properties of our domain
[0,1]^{2}
. (
26
) follows by case analysis.

1.

If
R_{s,a}>\nu
it implies that
p_{{\varepsilon}_{1},\nu}(\delta_{1})>\nu
and
p_{<{\varepsilon}_{0},\nu}(\delta_{0})>\nu
. By the definition of the polynomials
p_{\beta,\nu}
this implies that
\delta_{1}>{\varepsilon}_{1}
and
\delta_{0}<{\varepsilon}_{0}<{\varepsilon}_{1}/2
, which gives that
(\delta_{1}-\delta_{0})^{2}\geq{\varepsilon}_{1}^{2}/4
. Since
R_{s,a}
is always
\leq 1
we get:

R_{s,a}\leq 1\leq\frac{4(\delta_{1}-\delta_{0})^{2}}{{\varepsilon}_{1}^{2}}<\frac{4(\delta_{1}-\delta_{0})^{2}}{{\varepsilon}_{1}^{2}}+\nu.

2.

If
R_{s,a}\leq\nu
we get:

R_{s,a}\leq\frac{4(\delta_{1}-\delta_{0})^{2}}{{\varepsilon}_{1}^{2}}+\nu.

Hence rearranging the above and adding
\nu
on both sides gives (
26
). We can now apply Theorem
2.6
that shows that positive polynomials bounded away from
0
on the domain
[0,1]^{k}
have a bounded degree SoS proof to get:

\delta_{0},\delta_{1}\in[0,1]\vdash_{\exp(1/\nu)}R_{s,a}\leq\frac{4(\delta_{1}-\delta_{0})^{2}}{{\varepsilon}_{1}^{2}}+2\nu.

One can check that the rest of the proof is already SoS therefore we get:

\mathcal{A}_{I}\vdash_{\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)}\delta_{0},\delta_{1}\in[0,1]\vdash_{\exp(1/\nu)}\E_{a\sim{[n]\choose 1}}[R_{s,a}]\leq\frac{4\delta(G_{s})}{{\varepsilon}_{1}^{2}\ell}+2\nu.

One can do the exact same steps and use the SoS degree bound in Theorem
2.6
to get an SoS proof of the full statement with degree
\ell^{O(\ell^{i}/\nu)}
. The bound that comes from Theorem
2.6
is exponential in the degree of the polynomial
f
that we want to prove is SoS, and hence our degree bound is also
\exp(\ell^{i}/\nu)
. We believe there could be a more direct analysis to get a degree bound of
\mathrm{poly}(1/\nu,\ell^{i})
instead.
∎

We now move on to stating the edge covering theorem.
Below, we inspect the edges that stay inside a part in the shift partition, and show that they can nearly be encapsulated within subcubes on which some part becomes dense.

Theorem 4.3

(Edge-Covering Theorem)
.

For all constants
r\in\mathbb{N}
,
{\varepsilon}_{0},\ldots,{\varepsilon}_{r}\in[0,1]
, where
{\varepsilon}_{r}\leq\exp(-r)
and
{\varepsilon}_{0},\ldots,{\varepsilon}_{r}
satisfy that
{\varepsilon}_{i-1}\leq{\varepsilon}_{i}^{5}/(2^{6r})
, we get that:

\mathcal{A}_{I}\vdash_{\ell^{\mathrm{poly}(\ell^{r}/\nu)}}\mathrm{val}_{I}(X\wedge X^{\prime})\leq T_{0}(X,X^{\prime})+\ldots+T_{r}(X,X^{\prime})+\sf{err},

(27)

where:

\displaystyle T_{0}(X,X^{\prime})=\sum_{s}p_{{\varepsilon}_{0},\nu}(\delta(G_{s}))\E_{u\sim G}[G_{s}(u)\mathrm{val}_{u}(X\wedge X^{\prime})],

\displaystyle T_{i}(X,X^{\prime})=\ell^{i}\E_{a\in{[n]\choose i}}[\sum_{s}R_{s,a}\E_{u\sim J|_{a}}[G_{s}(u)\mathrm{val}^{a}_{u}(X\wedge X^{\prime})]]
