\displaystyle{\sf err}=4(1-\alpha)^{r+1}+2^{6r}\max_{i}\frac{{\varepsilon}_{i-1}}{{\varepsilon}_{i}^{4}}+|\Sigma|\ell^{O(r)}\sqrt{\nu}.

Proof.

We will first give a proof of this statement with the definition of
R_{s,a}
replaced by actual indicator functions in place of the polynomials
p_{\beta,\nu}
. Formally, for an
i\leq r
-restriction
a\in{[n]\choose i}
let
R_{s,a}
be an indicator variable that is 1 if
\delta(G_{s}|_{a})\geq{\varepsilon}_{i}
and
\delta(G_{s}|_{b})\leq{\varepsilon}_{|b|}
for all
b\subsetneq a
. We will then give a sketch of how to convert this into an SoS proof and work with the true definition of
R_{s,a}
.

For any set
S
let
\#sat(S)
denote the number of edges inside
S
that are satisfied by both
X
and
X^{\prime}
. We first have that:

\mathrm{val}_{I}(X\wedge X^{\prime})\leq\sum_{s}\frac{\#sat(G_{s})}{|E|},

(28)

since we know (from Fact
2.10
) that any edge that crosses between parts in the shift-partition must be violated by at least one of
X
or
X^{\prime}
. Let us therefore upper bound
\#sat(G_{s})
.

Consider the set indicated by
G_{s}
for fixed
s\in\Sigma
. Let the set of dense subcubes for
G_{s}
be defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{0}_{s}=\begin{cases}\{J\}~~~~~~~~~\text{if $\delta(G_{s})\geq{\varepsilon}_{0}$}\\
\phi~~~~~~~~~~~~\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{(1)}_{s}=\left\{a\in[n]:R_{s,a}=1\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{(r)}_{s}=\left\{a\in{[n]\choose r}:R_{s,a}=1\right\}.

Let
D_{s}
denote the dense part of
G_{s}
:
\cup_{i\leq r}\cup_{a\in\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{s}}G_{s}\cap J|_{a}
and let
H_{s}=G_{s}\setminus D_{s}
be the pseudorandom part of
G_{s}
. It is easy to check that
H_{s}
is
(r,{\varepsilon}_{r})
-pseudorandom. The edges inside
G_{s}
can be divided into four types of edges: edges that stay inside the dense subcubes, edges that go between two different dense subcubes, edges inside
H_{s}
and edges that go between
H_{s}
and
D_{s}
. Applying this subdivision of edges on
\#sat(G_{s})
we get that:

\displaystyle\frac{\#sat(G_{s})}{|E(J)|}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\leq r}\sum_{a\in\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{s}}\frac{\#sat(G_{s}\cap J|_{a})}{|E(J)|}+\frac{|E(H_{s},H_{s})|}{|E(J)|}+\frac{|E(H_{s},D_{s})|}{|E(J)|}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j\leq r}\sum\limits_{h=0}^{\min(i,j)}\sum_{a\in\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{s}}\sum_{H\subseteq a,|H|=h}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathcal{C}^{(j)}_{s}\\
a\neq b,a\cap b=H\end{subarray}}\frac{|E(J|_{a},J|_{b})|}{|E(J)|}.

(29)

We will check that the
i^{th}
-summand in the first term can be rearranged to give the
s^{th}
-summand in
T_{i}(X,X^{\prime})
. We have that:

\sum_{a\in\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{s}}\frac{\#sat(G_{s}\cap J|_{a})}{|E(J)|}=\frac{{[n]\choose i}|E(J|_{a})|}{|E(J)|}\E_{a\sim{[n]\choose i}}\left[R_{s,a}\frac{\#sat(G_{s}\cap J|_{a})}{|E(J|_{a})|}\right].

(30)

By a direct computation we get that
\frac{{[n]\choose i}|E(J|_{a})|}{|E(J)|}\leq\ell^{i}
. As for the second term above we get that,

\frac{\#sat(G_{s}\cap J|_{a})}{|E(J|_{a})|}\leq\frac{\sum_{u\in J|_{a}}\Ind[u\in G_{s}]\#sat_{a}(u)}{|V(J|_{a})|d_{a}}=\E_{u\in J|_{a}}[\Ind[u\in G_{s}]\mathrm{val}_{u}^{a}(X\wedge X^{\prime})],

where
d_{a}
denotes the degree of a vertex inside the subgraph
J|_{a}
and
\#sat_{a}(u)
denotes the number of edges in
J|_{a}
that are incident on
u
and are satisfied by both
X
and
X^{\prime}
. So plugging this into (
30
) and summing up over
i\leq r
we get that the first term in (
29
) gives us:

\sum_{i\leq r}\sum_{a\in\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{s}}\frac{\#sat(G_{s}\cap J|_{a})}{|E(J)|}\leq\sum_{i\leq r}\ell^{i}\E_{a\sim{[n]\choose i}}[R_{s,a}\E_{u\in J|_{a}}[\Ind[u\in G_{s}]\mathrm{val}_{u}^{a}(X\wedge X^{\prime})]].

(31)

We will bound the other terms in (
29
) by some small constant.

Bounding the last term in (
29
).

We focus on
i\leq j
, and in the end multiply the
bound we get by factor
2
.

If
R_{s,\phi}=1
, that is,
\delta(G_{s})\geq{\varepsilon}_{0}
then we can check that this is
0
since none of the other subcubes will have
R_{s,a}=1
. So let us assume that this is not the case.
Fix
i
and
H\subseteq i
of size
h
. For any
a\in{[n]\choose i},b\in{[n]\choose j}

intersecting in a set of size
h
we have that

\frac{|E(J|_{a},J|_{b})|}{|E(J)|}\leq\left(\frac{2\ell}{n}\right)^{i+j-h}
.
Indeed, to see this one can think of sampling an edge

(x,y)
in
J
uniformly at random, and asking what
is the probability that
a\subseteq x
and
b\subseteq y
. For that, we want
a\cap b
to be contained in
both
x
and
y
(which happens with probability at most
(2\ell/n)^{h}
), and that
a\setminus(a\cap b)\subseteq x
,
b\setminus(a\cap b)\subseteq y

which happens with probability at most
(2\ell/n)^{i-h}\cdot(2\ell/n)^{j-h}
.
Thus,
we get that the last term in (
29
)
is at most

\sum_{i\leq j\leq r}\sum\limits_{h=0}^{i-1}\sum_{a\in\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{s}}\sum_{H\subseteq a,|H|=h}|\{b~|b\in\mathcal{C}^{(j)}_{s},a\cap b=H,a\neq b\}|\left(\frac{2\ell}{n}\right)^{i+j-h}.

Here we note that
h
cannot be
i
, since then we
would be looking at sub-cubes
J|_{a}\subseteq J|_{b}
,
and so we cannot have both
R_{s,a}=1
and
R_{s,b}=1
.

Applying Claim
4.2
on the
restriction
G_{s}|_{H}
, we get that

|\{b~|b\in\mathcal{C}^{(j)}_{s},a\cap b=H,a\neq b\}|\leq\frac{n^{j-h}}{\ell^{j-h}}\frac{\delta(G_{s}|_{H})}{{\varepsilon}_{j}^{2}}\leq\frac{n^{j-h}}{\ell^{j-h}}\frac{{\varepsilon}_{h}}{{\varepsilon}_{j}^{2}}.

We used the fact that as
R_{s,b}=1
,
G_{s}
is at most
{\varepsilon}_{h}
dense in
J|_{h}
. Plugging that above we get that the last term
on the right hand side of (
29
) is
at most

\sum_{i\leq j\leq r}\sum\limits_{h=0}^{i-1}\sum_{a\in\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{s}}\sum_{H\subseteq a,|H|=h}\frac{n^{j-h}}{\ell^{j-h}}\frac{{\varepsilon}_{h}}{{\varepsilon}_{j}^{2}}\leq\sum_{i,j\leq r}2^{i}\cdot i\cdot\frac{n^{j-h}}{\ell^{j-h}}\frac{{\varepsilon}_{h}}{{\varepsilon}_{j}^{2}}\cdot|\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{s}|\left(\frac{2\ell}{n}\right)^{i+j-h},

and as
|\mathcal{C}^{(i)}_{s}|\leq\frac{\delta(G_{s})n^{i}}{{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}\ell^{i}}
due to
Claim
4.2
, we get further upper
bound this by

\sum_{i,j\leq r}2^{i}\cdot i\cdot\frac{n^{i+j-h}}{\ell^{i+j-h}}\frac{{\varepsilon}_{h}\delta(G_{s})}{{\varepsilon}_{j}^{2}{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}}\left(\frac{2\ell}{n}\right)^{i+j-h}\leq 2^{3r+1}r^{2}\delta(G_{s})\max_{i}\frac{{\varepsilon}_{i-1}}{{\varepsilon}_{i}^{4}}\leq 2^{6r}\max_{i}\frac{{\varepsilon}_{i-1}}{{\varepsilon}_{i}^{4}}\delta(G_{s}).

Summing this over
s
gives that the contribution of the last term in (
29
) to (
28
) is at most

2^{6r}\max_{i}\frac{{\varepsilon}_{i-1}}{{\varepsilon}_{i}^{4}}
.

Bounding the second and third term in (
29
).

Here, we use Theorem
3.17
.
The point is that the set
H_{s}
is pseudo-random,
and hence we can get good control over the edges from it to other sets.

More precisely, for the second term note that
H_{s}
is
(r,{\varepsilon}_{r})
-pseudorandom as per Definition
3.16
,
so by Theorem
3.17
) with
\gamma={\varepsilon}_{r}
we get:

\frac{|E(H_{s})|}{|E(J)|}=\delta(H_{s})-\langle H_{s},LH_{s}\rangle\leq 2\delta(H_{s})(1-\alpha)^{r+1}\leq 2\delta(G_{s})(1-\alpha)^{r+1},

(32)

where the first inequality holds as long as
{\varepsilon}_{r}\leq(1-\alpha)^{r+1}\exp(-r)
. Note that the second term in the structure theorem
q_{a}(H_{s})(\delta(H_{s}|_{a})-{\varepsilon}_{r})
is non-positive since
q_{a}
is SoS and
H_{s}
is pseudorandom. The third term corresponding to Booleanity,
B(H_{s})
is
0
since
H_{s}^{3}(u)=H_{s}(u)
.
Summing up over
s
yields that the contribution of this to the right hand side of (
28
)
is at most
2(1-\alpha)^{r+1}
.

The bound on the third term in (
29
) is similar. We have that

\frac{|E(H_{s},D_{s})|}{|E(J)|}=\langle D_{s},JH_{s}\rangle\leq\sqrt{\langle D_{s},D_{s}\rangle\cdot\langle H_{s},J^{2}H_{s}\rangle}\leq 2\delta(G_{s})(1-\alpha)^{r+1}.

(33)

In the last inequality, we used
\langle D_{S},D_{s}\rangle\leq\delta(G_{s})
and

\langle H_{s},J^{2}H_{s}\rangle\leq 2\delta(G_{s})(1-\alpha)^{2(r+1)}

which follows by Theorem
3.17

exactly as above. Summing up over
s
yields total contribution of at most
2(1-\alpha)^{r+1}
.

SoS-ing the proof:

We give a proof sketch of how to convert this into an SoS proof. To avoid Booleanity error we will apply (
4.1
) multiple times with
R_{s,a}
replaced with
p_{\beta,\nu}
’s and take an average. We know that
R_{s,a}=p_{1}(\delta_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot p_{t}(\delta_{t})
where
p_{i}
’s are approximate indicators
p_{\beta_{i},\nu}
(or
p_{<\beta_{i},\nu}
) and
\delta_{i}
’s are of the form
\delta(G_{s}|_{b})
for
b\subseteq a
. Then define
R^{c}_{s,a}
as
p^{c}_{1}(\delta_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot p^{c}_{t}(\delta_{t})
where
p^{c}_{i}(\delta_{i})=p_{\beta_{i}+c\nu^{2},\nu^{2}}
(resp.
p_{<\beta_{i}+c\nu^{2},\nu^{2}}
) for all
c\in\{0,\ldots,\lfloor 1/\nu\rfloor\}
.

Note that
G_{s}
’s and the functions
\#sat(G_{s}\cap J|_{a})
are actual indicators as written since we can express
G_{s}(u)=\Ind[X_{s}-X^{\prime}_{u}=s]
and
\Ind[e\text{ is satisfied }]
as a polynomial in
(X,X^{\prime})
. Define
H^{c}_{s}(u)=G_{s}(u)\prod_{a\subset u:|a|\leq r}(1-R^{c}_{s,a})
and
D^{c}_{s}=G_{s}(u)(1-H^{c}_{s,a})
. Defining dense subcubes with
R^{c}_{s,a}
we get the following analogue of (
29
):

\displaystyle\frac{\#sat(G_{s})}{|E(J)|}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\leq r}\sum_{a\in{[n]\choose i}}R^{c}_{s,a}\frac{\#sat(G_{s}\cap J|_{a})}{|E(J)|}+\langle H^{c}_{s},AH^{c}_{s}\rangle+\langle D^{c}_{s},JH^{c}_{s}\rangle

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j\leq r}\sum\limits_{h=0}^{\min(i,j)}\sum_{a\in{[n]\choose i}}R^{c}_{s,a}\sum_{H\subseteq a,|H|=h}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in{[n]\choose j}\\
a\neq b,a\cap b=H\end{subarray}}R^{c}_{s,b}\frac{|E(J|_{a},J|_{b})|}{|E(J)|}.

(34)

We then take an expectation over
c\in\{0,\ldots,\lfloor 1/\nu\rfloor\}
and then a sum over
s\in\Sigma
.

Now we can use the same analysis to upper bound each of these terms. The analysis for the first term follows as is; we get an
\E_{c}[R^{c}_{s,a}]
(instead of
R_{s,a}
) but we will ignore this slight difference as it does not affect our final analysis of the algorithm.
For the second term we use the SoS version of Claim
4.2
(as stated) to say that
\E_{a\in{[n]\choose i}}[R^{c}_{s,a}]
is small and the same tricks like those in the SoS-ization of Claim
4.2
. For the third and fourth terms we apply the structure theorem on the almost-Boolean function
H_{s}
and bound the Booleanity term
\E_{c}[\sum_{s}B(H^{c}_{s})]
by
|\Sigma|\ell^{O(r)}\sqrt{\nu}
instead. Additionally in the fourth term we apply an SoS version of Cauchy-Schwarz. We omit these details as we’ve carried out similar arguments before, but formally prove the upper bound on
\E_{c}[\sum_{s}B(H^{c}_{s})]
in Claim
B.1
in the appendix.
∎

4.2
The Main Lemma

In this section, we consider UG instances that have completeness bounded away from
1
and possibly very close to
0
. The analysis of our algorithm differs only in one step: concluding that the shift potential is large.

The following claim asserts that taking two assignments
X,X^{\prime}
from our pseudo-distribution,
the expected number of edges that are satisfied by
both
X
and
X^{\prime}
is still bounded away from
0
:

Claim 4.4
.

\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[\mathrm{val}_{I}(X\wedge X^{\prime})]\geq c^{2}
.

\displaystyle\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[\mathrm{val}_{I}(X\wedge X^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[\E_{e\sim E(G)}[\mathbb{1}(e\text{ satisfied by }X)\mathbb{1}(e\text{ satisfied by }X^{\prime})]]

\displaystyle=\E_{e\sim E(G)}[\pPr_{\mu}[e\text{ satisfied by }X]^{2}]

\displaystyle\geq\E_{e\sim E(G)}[\pPr_{\mu}[e\text{ satisfied by }X]]^{2}~~~~~~~~~~\text{(by Cauchy-Schwarz)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{val}_{\mu}(I)^{2}.

Lemma 4.5
.

For all positive constants
c,\alpha,\tau\leq 1
,
\nu=\frac{c^{4}}{|\Sigma|^{2}\ell^{O(r)}}
, all integers
\ell\geq\Omega(r),n\geq\ell
, where
r=\Theta(\frac{\log c}{\log(1-\alpha)})
the following holds: Let
I
be an affine UG instance on
J_{n,\ell,\alpha}
and
\mu
be a pseudodistribution over assignments for
I
with
\mathrm{val}_{\mu}(I)\geq c
and degree at least
\frac{\ell^{O(r)}\mathrm{poly}(|\Sigma|)}{\mathrm{poly}(c)\tau}+\ell^{\mathrm{poly}(|\Sigma|\ell^{r}/c)}
. Then there exists a restriction
a\subseteq[n]
of size
i\leq r
, a degree
\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)
polynomial
P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})
in a fixed set of
|\Sigma|
polynomials, subsets
A,B\subseteq V(J|_{a})
of size at most
\frac{\ell^{O(i)}|\Sigma|^{2}}{c^{2}\tau}
and strings
y_{A},y_{B}
such that conditioning
\mu
on the events
Y_{A}=y_{A}
and
Y_{B}=y_{B}
gives degree
\widetilde{\Omega}(1/\nu)
pseudodistributions
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
such that:

1.

\mathcal{A}_{I}~~\vdash_{\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)}~~P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})\in[0,1]
.

2.

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[\Phi^{a}_{\beta,\nu}(X,X^{\prime})P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})]\geq\frac{c^{\Omega(r)}}{\exp(r^{2})}\pE[P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})]
.

3.

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})]\geq\Omega\left(\frac{c^{2}}{8r\ell^{i}|\Sigma|}\right)
.

4.

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},v_{1}\sim S:u_{1}\neq v_{1}\\
u_{2},v_{2}\sim S:u_{2}\neq v_{2}\end{subarray}}[I(Y_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y_{u_{2},v_{2}})]\leq\tau.

5.

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},v_{1}\sim S:u_{1}\neq v_{1}\\
u_{2},v_{2}\sim S:u_{2}\neq v_{2}\end{subarray}}[I(Y^{\prime}_{u_{1},v_{1}};Y^{\prime}_{u_{2},v_{2}})]\leq\tau,

where
\beta=\left(\frac{\mathrm{poly}(c)}{\exp(r)}\right)^{r-i+1}
,
S=V(J|_{a})
,
Y_{u,v}=(X_{u},X_{v},p_{u},p_{v})
,
Y^{\prime}_{u,v}=(X^{\prime}_{u},X^{\prime}_{v},p^{\prime}_{u},p^{\prime}_{v})
and the mutual information is taken with respect to the collection of local distributions
\mathcal{L}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2},p_{\beta,\nu})
.

Proof.

Our strategy will be to use the edge-covering theorem. We will apply
\pE_{\mu\times\mu}
on both sides of the inequality and use Claim
4.4
to say that
\pE[\mathrm{val}_{I}(X\wedge X^{\prime})]
is large.

We will set parameters in the end of the proof such that:
r
is large enough so that
4(1-\alpha)^{r+1}\leq c^{2}/6
,
\nu
is small enough such that
|\Sigma|\ell^{O(r)}\sqrt{\nu}\leq c^{2}/6
and
{\varepsilon}_{i}
’s satisfy
2^{6r}\max_{i}\frac{{\varepsilon}_{i-1}}{{\varepsilon}_{i}^{4}}\leq c^{2}/6
and further
{\varepsilon}_{i}
’s satisfy the conditions of Theorem
4.3
. So we get that the error term in Theorem
4.3
is small,

{\sf err}=4(1-\alpha)^{r+1}+2^{6r}\max_{i}\frac{{\varepsilon}_{i-1}}{{\varepsilon}_{i}^{4}}+|\Sigma|\ell^{O(r)}\sqrt{\nu}\leq\frac{c^{2}}{2}.

(35)

Applying
\pE_{\mu\times\mu}
on both sides of Theorem
4.3
(assuming
\deg(\mu)\geq\ell^{\mathrm{poly}(\ell^{r}/\nu)}
) and
using Claim
4.4
to lower bound pseudoexpectation of the LHS we get:

c^{2}\leq\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[\mathrm{val}_{I}(X\wedge X^{\prime})]\leq\sum_{i\in[r]}\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[T_{i}(X,X^{\prime})]+{\sf err}\leq\sum_{i\in[r]}\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[T_{i}(X,X^{\prime})]+\frac{c^{2}}{2},

Therefore we get that there is an
i\in[r]
for which
\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[T_{i}(X,X^{\prime})]\geq c^{2}/2r
, and so

\pE_{\mu\times\mu}\E_{a\sim{[n]\choose i}}[\sum_{s}R_{s,a}\E_{u\sim J|_{a}}[G_{s}(u)\mathrm{val}^{a}_{u}(X\wedge X^{\prime})]]\geq\frac{c^{2}}{2r\ell^{i}}.

(36)

We aim to condition
\pE_{\mu\times\mu}
on an appropriate polynomial (as in the proof of Lemma
3.20
) so that the resulting pseudoexpectation operator satisfies:
\pE^{\prime}[\E_{u\sim J|_{a}}[G_{s}(u)\mathrm{val}^{a}_{u}(X\wedge X^{\prime})]]]\geq\Omega(c^{2}/r)
for some specific
i
-restriction
a
. To do so we apply
\pE_{\mu\times\mu}
to Claim
4.2
and sum up over
s
, to get

\pE_{\mu\times\mu}\left[\E_{a\sim{[n]\choose i}}\sum_{s}R_{s,a}\right]\leq\frac{4}{{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}\ell^{i}}+O(|\Sigma|\nu)\leq\frac{5}{{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}\ell_{i}},

where for the last inequality we have assumed
\nu\leq O(1/\ell^{i}|\Sigma|)
.

We split the RHS in equation
36
to
c^{2}/4r\ell^{i}+c^{2}/4r\ell^{i}
, where in the first term we further use the above equation:
1/\ell^{i}\geq\frac{{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}}{5}\pE_{\mu\times\mu}[\E_{a\sim{[n]\choose i}}[\sum_{s}R_{s,a}]]
to get that,

\pE_{\mu\times\mu}\E_{a\sim{[n]\choose i}}\left[\sum_{s}R_{s,a}\E_{u\sim J|_{a}}[G_{s}(u)\mathrm{val}^{a}_{u}(X\wedge X^{\prime})]\right]\geq\frac{c^{2}{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}}{20r}\pE_{\mu\times\mu}\left[\E_{a\sim{[n]\choose i}}[\sum_{s}R_{s,a}]\right]+\frac{c^{2}}{4r\ell^{i}},

and by re-arranging

\pE_{\mu\times\mu}\E_{a\sim{[n]\choose i}}\left[\sum_{s}R_{s,a}\left(\E_{u\sim J|_{a}}[G_{s}(u)\mathrm{val}^{a}_{u}(X\wedge X^{\prime})]-\frac{c^{2}{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}}{20r}\right)\right]\geq\frac{c^{2}}{4r\ell^{i}}.

By averaging we get an
a\in{[n]\choose i},s\in\Sigma
such that:

\pE_{\mu\times\mu}\left[R_{s,a}\left(\E_{u\sim J|_{a}}[G_{s}(u)\mathrm{val}^{a}_{u}(X\wedge X^{\prime})]-\frac{c^{2}{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}}{20r}\right)\right]\geq\frac{c^{2}}{4r\ell^{i}|\Sigma|}.

(37)

The last result is the same as equation (
10
)
in the proof of Lemma
3.20
,
which is the analogous statement for the completeness s clsoe to
1
case. The rest of the proof is essentially the same as the rest of the proof of Lemma
3.20
, and is outlined below.

Applying Lemma
3.19
we get pseudodistributions
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
with low global correlation (as required in points 4,5 of the Lemma) with:

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}\left[R_{s,a}\left(\E_{u\sim J|_{a}}[G_{s}(u)\mathrm{val}^{a}_{u}(X\wedge X^{\prime})]-\frac{c^{2}{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}}{20r}\right)\right]\geq\frac{c^{2}}{8r\ell^{i}|\Sigma|}.

(38)

Letting
P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})=R_{s,a}(X,X^{\prime})
it is easy to check that:

\mathcal{A}_{I}\vdash_{\widetilde{O}(\exp(i)/\nu)}P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})\in[0,1],\text{ and }~~~\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}}[P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})]\geq\frac{c^{2}}{8r\ell^{i}|\Sigma|},

which proves points 1 and 3 of the Lemma statement. The only thing remaining to check is that conditioning
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
on
R_{s,a}
results in large shift-partition potential on
J|_{a}
. Recall that the shift-partition potential on
J|_{a}
is:
\phi^{a}_{\beta,\nu}(X,X^{\prime})=\sum_{s}\E_{u\sim J|_{a}}[G_{s}(u)p_{\beta,\nu}(\mathrm{val}^{a}_{u}(X))p_{\beta,\nu}(\mathrm{val}^{a}_{u}(X^{\prime}))]^{2}
. From equation (
38
) we have that conditioning
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
on
R_{s,a}
gives:

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|R_{s,a}}[\E_{u\sim J|_{a}}[G_{s}(u)\mathrm{val}^{a}_{u}(X\wedge X^{\prime})]]\geq\frac{c^{2}{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}}{20r}.

We can prove that for
x\in[0,1]\vdash_{\widetilde{O}(1/\nu)}x\leq p_{\beta,\nu}(x)+\beta+O(\nu)
(using the standard machinery in Section
2.3.2
). Applying this with
\mathrm{val}^{a}_{u}(X\wedge X^{\prime})
and
\beta=\frac{c^{2}{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}}{40r}
, and further noting that

p_{\beta,\nu}(\mathrm{val}_{u}^{a}(X\wedge X^{\prime}))\leq p_{\beta,\nu}(\mathrm{val}_{u}^{a}(X))p_{\beta,\nu}(\mathrm{val}_{u}^{a}(X^{\prime}))+O(\nu)
we get:

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|R_{s,a}}[\E_{u\sim J|_{a}}[G_{s}(u)p_{\beta,\nu}(\mathrm{val}_{u}^{a}(X))p_{\beta,\nu}(\mathrm{val}_{u}^{a}(X^{\prime}))]]\geq\frac{c^{2}{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}}{40r}-O(\nu).

Applying Cauchy-Schwarz we get:

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|R_{s,a}}[\E_{u\sim J|_{a}}[G_{s}(u)p_{\beta,\nu}(\mathrm{val}_{u}^{a}(X))p_{\beta,\nu}(\mathrm{val}_{u}^{a}(X^{\prime}))]^{2}]\geq\Omega\left(\frac{c^{4}{\varepsilon}_{i}^{4}}{r^{2}}\right).

By adding the terms corresponding to other
s\in\Sigma
we immediately get:

\pE_{\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|R_{s,a}}[\phi_{\beta,\nu}^{a}(X,X^{\prime})]\geq\Omega\left(\frac{c^{4}{\varepsilon}_{i}^{4}}{r^{2}}\right).

(39)

We finish the proof by setting
r,{\varepsilon}_{i},\nu
according to all the requirements above.

Setting parameters:

To make
\sf{err}
in (
35
) small we set
r=\Theta(\frac{\log c}{\log(1-\alpha)})
,
\nu=\frac{c^{4}}{|\Sigma|^{2}\ell^{O(r)}}
,
{\varepsilon}_{j-1}={\varepsilon}_{j}^{5}/2^{6r}
with
{\varepsilon}_{r}=\Theta(c^{2}\exp(-r))
. We need
\beta=c^{2}{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}/40r
which equals
(\mathrm{poly}(c)/\exp(r))^{r-i+1}
. We can set
\deg(\mu)=\ell^{\mathrm{poly}(\ell^{r}/\nu)}=\ell^{\mathrm{poly}(|\Sigma|\ell^{r}/c)}
so that it is valid to apply the edge-covering theorem. Additionally when we apply Lemma
3.19
we need
\deg(\mu)\geq\Omega(\log|\Sigma|/p\tau\nu)
where
p=c^{2}/4r\ell^{i}|\Sigma|
so it suffices to take
\deg(\mu)=\frac{\ell^{O(r)}\mathrm{poly}(|\Sigma|)}{\mathrm{poly}(c)\tau}
. One can check that these parameters are good enough to make the rest of the statements in the proof work out. From (
39
) we get that the conditioned shift-partition potential is at least
\Omega(c^{2}{\varepsilon}_{i}^{4}/r^{2})\geq\frac{c^{O(r)}}{\exp(r)}
and this completes the proof.
∎

4.3
Proof of Theorem
1.6

The algorithm for arbitrary completeness is the same as that for completeness close to
1
albeit with different parameter settings for
r,\delta,D,\gamma
for the main algorithm and
t,\tau,\beta,\nu
for SubRound. Given Lemma
4.5
the rest of the analysis of the algorithm remains exactly the same. We set these parameters below but omit the description of the algorithm. The guarantees of this algorithm are potentially worse (running time
\approx n^{\ell^{\mathrm{poly}(\ell)}}
and approximation factor
\approx c^{O(\log 1/c)}
) than the one for completeness close to
1
, but the important thing to note is that the running time is polynomial in
n
(as long as
\ell,|\Sigma|
are
O(1)
) and the approximation factor only depends on
c
and not on
\ell,n
or
|\Sigma|
.

Theorem 4.6
.

For all positive constants
c\in(0,1]
,
\alpha\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, all integers
\ell\geq\Omega(r)
with
\alpha\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
r=\Theta(\frac{\log c}{\log(1-\alpha)})
and
n
large enough, Algorithm
3.4
(with modified parameter settings given below) has the following guarantee: If
I
is an instance of affine Unique Games on the
(n,\ell,\alpha)
-Johnson graph
J
with alphabet
\Sigma
and
\mathrm{val}(I)=c
, then in time
|V(J)|^{D}
Algorithm
3.4
returns an
\Omega\left(\frac{c^{\Omega(r)}}{\exp(r^{2})}\right)
-satisfying assignment for
I
, with
D=\frac{\ell^{\mathrm{poly}(|\Sigma|\ell^{r}/c)}}{c^{O(r)}}
.

Proof.

Let
r=\Theta(\frac{\log c}{\log(1-\alpha)})
. Given the
i\leq r
-restricted subcube
a\subseteq[n],P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})
and
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
from Lemma
4.5
we have that
\Phi_{\beta,\nu}^{a}(\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}|P_{a})\geq\frac{c^{\Omega(r)}}{\exp(r^{2})}
, therefore we apply Lemma
3.10
with
S=J|_{a}
, the polynomial
E(X,X^{\prime})=P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})
and the parameters
\beta=\left(\frac{\mathrm{poly}(c)}{\exp(r)}\right)^{r-i+1},\nu=\frac{c^{4}}{|\Sigma|^{2}\ell^{O(r)}},\delta=\frac{c^{O(r)}}{\exp(r^{2})},p=\Omega\left(\frac{c^{2}}{8r\ell^{i}|\Sigma|}\right)
and
\tau=\frac{c^{O(r)}}{\exp(r^{2})\ell^{O(i)}|\Sigma|}
. The parameter
\tau
has been chosen so that
O(\frac{\sqrt{\tau}+1/|S|}{p\delta^{2}})=\frac{c^{O(r)}}{\exp(r^{2})}
and therefore we can apply the rounding lemma (Lemma
3.11
) with
\zeta=\frac{c^{O(r)}}{\exp(r^{2})}
and
\gamma=\frac{c^{\Omega(r)}}{\exp(r^{2})}
and the same settings of
\beta,\nu,\delta
. This shows that there exists a subcube
a\in{[n]\choose i}
with SubRound value that is at least
\frac{c^{\Omega(r)}}{\exp(r^{2})}
if
\deg(\mu)\geq\frac{\ell^{O(r)}\mathrm{poly}(|\Sigma|)}{\mathrm{poly}(c)\tau}+\ell^{\mathrm{poly}(|\Sigma|\ell^{r}/c)}
. Hence it suffices to have degree of
\mu
equal to
D=\frac{\ell^{\mathrm{poly}(|\Sigma|\ell^{r}/c)}}{c^{O(r)}}
.

By using SubRound as a subroutine, we finish the proof of this theorem by applying the iteration Lemma
3.12
with completeness
c
,
\gamma=c^{2}
,
\delta=\frac{c^{\Omega(r)}}{\exp(r^{2})}
and
r=\Theta(\frac{\log c}{\log(1-\alpha)})
so that
(1-\alpha)^{r}=\mathrm{poly}(c)
. We can check that SubRound (with the parameter settings above) satisfies the hypotheses of the lemma: it finds a
\leq r
-restricted subcube and an assignment to it with value
\Omega(\delta)
in time
|V(J)|^{D}
(where
D
is the degree bound above). This gives us that Algorithm
3.4
outputs an assignment of value at least
\Omega\left(\frac{c^{\Omega(r)}}{\exp(r^{2})}\right)
in time
|V(G)|^{O(D)}
.
∎

5
Affine Unique Games on other Globally Hypercontractive Graphs

In this section, we explain how to adapt our techniques from Sections
3
,
4
for
general globally hypercontractive graphs. We first give a semi-formal definition of global hypercontractivity and go over the steps of our algorithm, noting that even though we stated it for Johnson graphs earlier, it works for general graphs as long as we have an SoS certificate of global hypercontractivity. Later we give the specifics for the proofs of Theorems
1.7
and
1.8
: UG algorithms for the Grassmann graph and random walks over HDXs.

5.1
The Components that Go Into the Algorithm

The
4
-components that are used in
our algorithm proving Theorem
1.6

and the interaction between them can be abstracted as follows.

1.

Global hypercontractivity.

The first component going into our algorithm is a global hypercontractive inequality (or a consequence of it).
This is a result asserting that
for our underlying graph
G
, for all
\gamma>0
there are
r\in\mathbb{N}
,

{\varepsilon}_{0},\ldots,{\varepsilon}_{r},\zeta,\eta>0
and a collection

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{0}\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{C}_{r}
of sets of vertices (these are the combinations
of the basic sets) satisfying the following properties:

(a)

\mathcal{C}
consists of non-expanding sets:

The edge expansion of each
C\in\mathcal{C}
in
G
is
1-\zeta
.

(b)

\mathcal{C}
explains all small sets that
do not expand well.
A basic form of this property
is that sets
S\subseteq V(G)
that are not dense in
any set in
\mathcal{C}
, in the sense that
\delta(S\cap C)\leq{\varepsilon}_{1}\delta(C)

for all
C\in\mathcal{C}
, have expansion close to
1
, that is,
\Phi(S)\geq 1-\gamma
.

A stronger version of this property, that often follows from the above (as is the case of all graphs of interest in this paper) and that is used in the proof of Theorem
1.5
, is that
one can capture a constant fraction of
S
using
C\in\mathcal{C}
in which
S
is dense.
Equivalently, this property says that for a set of vertices
S\subseteq V(G)
, taking
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\{C\in\mathcal{C}~|~C\in\mathcal{C}_{i},~\delta(S\cap C)\geq{\varepsilon}_{i}\delta(C)\}
and defining
C^{\prime}=\bigcup_{C\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}C
, if
\delta(S\cap C^{\prime})\leq\eta\delta(S)
then

\Phi(S)\geq 1-\gamma
.

An even stronger form of global hypercontractivity, that again follows from the most basic form in our cases of interest, is the edge covering theorem.
We used this form of global hypercontracitivity in the proof of Theorem
1.6
.
Informally, this result says that not only a substantial chunk of
S
can be covered by non-expanding sets from
\mathcal{C}
in which
S
is dense, but in fact one can capture almost all of the edges staying inside
S
using
C\in\mathcal{C}
. More precisely,
this result asserts that
we may take
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\prime}

such that the following properties hold:

i.

Edge covering:
sampling an edge
(u,v)
inside
S
, we have that
except with probability
\xi
it holds that
(u,v)

is an edge inside some
C^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
.

ii.

Maximally dense: in words, this means that
every
C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
has no
C\supsetneq C^{\prime\prime}

in
\mathcal{C}
in which
S
is somewhat dense.
Formally, we require that for all
i=1,\ldots,r
,

C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}\cap\mathcal{C}_{i}
,

j<i
and
C_{j}\in\mathcal{C}_{j}
such that
C_{j}\supseteq C^{\prime\prime}
, it holds that

\delta(S\cap C_{j})<{\varepsilon}_{j}\delta(C_{j})
.

The edge covering theorem allows us to argue that in our shift partition

F_{s}(X,X^{\prime})=\{v\in V~|~X(v)-X^{\prime}(v)=s\}
we may capture
almost all edges that stay within some
F_{s}
using the basic
sets from
\mathcal{C}
. In particular, since edges that both
X
and
X^{\prime}
satisfy constitute a constant fraction of edges and they all stay within some
F_{s}
, it means that we may capture almost all of them using sets from
\mathcal{C}
.

2.

By elementary arguments, it follows
that there is such a
C\in\mathcal{C}
and
s
such
that
F_{s}

becomes dense in
C
with noticeable probability and inside
C
a constant fraction of the edges are satisfied by both
X
and
X^{\prime}
. In our argument we consider this event
(this was approximated by the polynomial
P_{a}(X,X^{\prime})
in our algorithm to stay within
the realm of SoS). As we explained in the introduction
this conditioning may create correlation between
X
and

X^{\prime}
that would cause our rounding technique not to work.

To circumvent this, we showed that:

(a)

Eliminating global correlations:
using conditioning (as in
[
RT12
]
) we are able to make sure that the two collection
of random variables
\{Z_{u,v}\}_{u,v\in C},\{Z^{\prime}_{u,v}\}_{u,v\in C}
of interest
(that are concerned with local information about the assignments
X
and
X^{\prime}
on vertices
u,v
) are roughly
pairwise independent.

(b)

Conditioning mostly preserves independence:
As the probability of the event that

C
becomes dense in
F_{s}
is noticeable, and
\{Z_{u,v}\}_{u,v\in C}
,
\{Z^{\prime}_{u,v}\}_{u,v\in C}
were pairwise-independent to begin with, we argue
that it cannot create too many dependencies between

(Z_{u,v},Z^{\prime}_{u,v})
.

3.

Rounding.
We then gave a rounding procedure on a subcube
C
and a pseudodistribution
\mathcal{D}
, where on average (over
\mathcal{D}
) our supposed assignments
X,X^{\prime}
satisfy a constant fraction of the edges (formalized via the notion of
shift-partition potential), some part
F_{s}
of the shift partition is large, and on which we have near
independence between certain random variables

\{(Z_{u,v},Z^{\prime}_{u,v})\}_{u,v}
. The output of the rounding procedure is an assignment to the vertices of
C
that satisfies a constant fraction of the constraints inside it.

4.

An iteration result.
By that, we mean a result saying that if we have a procedure as above that is able to find a
C\in\mathcal{C}
and find an assignment to the vertices of
C
that satisfies a constant fraction of the constraints in it, then we can iterate it to satisfy a constant fraction
of the constraints in the whole graph.

This is achieved by randomizing the edges incident to
C
, which ensures that any subsequent
C^{\prime}
we find can only have negligible overlap with the
C
’s we have found thus far.
The idea is that any
C
that we found has at least

\delta
of the edges touching it staying inside it,
out of which we manage to satisfy a constant fraction,
hence overall we manage to satisfy a constant fraction
of the edges touching
C
. In return for that,
we randomize the edges touching
C
hence effectively giving up on the objective value coming from those edges. The point is that since the ratio between the number of edges we manage to satisfy, and the number of edges we give up on is
\Omega(1)
, after a few iterations we will manage to satisfy a
constant fraction of the edges before we drop the objective value of our instance by too much.

Our of these
4
components, the second, third and
fourth components work as is for any of the graphs that we
care about. Indeed, the elimination of correlations and
conditioning are completely generic, and the rounding
procedure only hinges on the constraints being affine.
As for the fourth component, it only relies on the properties
of the family
\mathcal{C}
guaranteed from our structure
theorem and otherwise is also generic.

We next discuss the first component, which is a globally
hypercontractive inequality, and for concreteness we
consider the most basic version of it. This is a result
saying that a set
S
which is not dense inside any

C\in\mathcal{C}
has large expansion, and in
all of our graphs of interest such results are known

[
KMMS18
,
KMS18
,
FKLM20
,
KLLM21
,
BHKL22b
,
GLL22
]
.
For such a result to give us an algorithm, we also require
the proof of this part to be in the SoS proof system of
constant degree (which is the case in all of the above
examples, but in principle may not be the case).

In the case of the Johnson graph the collection

\mathcal{C}
is simply the collection of basic sets

\mathcal{C}=\left\{J|_{a}~|~|a|\leq r\right\},\qquad\text{where }J|_{a}=\left\{u\in\binom{[n]}{\ell}~\Big|~u\supseteq a\right\},

the basic form of the global hypercontractive inequality is
from
[
KMMS18
]
(in the appendix we give an SoS version of
that proof), and we have shown in Section
4
how to conclude from it the edge covering theorem in a black-box manner.

5.2
The Algorithm for the Grassmann Graph

We now explain the first component above
for the Grassmann graph, which is established in
[
KMS18
]
. Recall that the Grassmann graph

{\sf Grass}(n,\ell)
is the graph whose vertices are all

\ell
-dimensional subspaces
L\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
,
and
(L,L^{\prime})
is an edge if
{\sf dim}(L\cap L^{\prime})=\ell-1
.

Inside the Grassmann graph
{\sf Grass}(n,\ell)
we have the two basic sets

Z_{x}=\left\{L~|~x\in L\right\}\text{ for $x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}$},\qquad Z^{\prime}_{W}=\left\{L~|~L\subseteq W\right\}\text{ for
a hyperplane $W\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}$},

and using them we can define the collections
\mathcal{C}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{r}
and
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{0}\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{C}_{r}
as above. Indeed,
for a subspace
X\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
we define

Z_{X}=\cap_{x\in X}Z_{x}
and for a subspace
W\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
we define
Z^{\prime}_{W}=\cap_{W^{\prime}\supseteq W}Z_{W^{\prime}}

and then

\mathcal{C}_{m}=\bigcup_{m_{1}+m_{2}=m}\left\{Z_{X}\cap Z^{\prime}_{W}~\Big|{\sf dim}(X)=m_{1},{\sf dim}(W)=n-m_{2},X\subseteq W\right\}.

The result of
[
KMS18
]
shows that for all
\gamma>0
,
there are
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
{\varepsilon}>0

such that if
S
is a set of
vertices in
{\sf Grass}(n,\ell)
such that
\delta(S\cap C)\leq{\varepsilon}\delta(C)
for all
C\in\mathcal{C}
, then
\Phi(S)\geq 1-\gamma
. In fact, the same proof shows that a slightly more general statement holds for bounded functions,
asserting that if
F\colon V({\sf Grass}(n,\ell))\to[0,1]
satisfies that

\E_{x}[F(x)^{2}1_{x\in C}]\leq{\varepsilon}\E_{x}[1_{x\in C}]
, then

\langle F,TF\rangle\geq(1-\gamma)\E_{x}[F^{2}(x)].

where
T
is the normalized adjacency matrix of
{\sf Grass}(n,\ell)
. Moreover their proof can be seen
to be SoS-able since each step uses polynomial inequalities like the Parceval, Cauchy-Schwarz and Hölder’s inequality on the variables
F
. We have used precisely these inequalities in Section
C
to get an SoS certificate of global hypercontractivity for the Johnson graph (Theorem
3.17
). In fact,
[
KMS18
]
’s proof is very similar to that of
[
KMMS18
]
for the Johnson graph therefore one can similarly get an SoS certificate for the Grassmann.

From this result, one can follow along the lines of Theorem
4.3
to deduce,
in a black-box manner, an edge covering theorem for
the Grassmann graph:

Theorem 5.1
.

For all
\eta>0
there
are
r\in\mathbb{N}
and

{\varepsilon}_{0},\ldots,{\varepsilon}_{r}>0
such that
given a set

S\subseteq{\sf Grass}(n,\ell)

one may find
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{i}

such that

1.

For each
i
and
C\in\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}
it holds
that
\delta(S\cap C)\geq{\varepsilon}_{i}\delta(C)
.

2.

For
\tilde{S}=\bigcup_{i=0}^{r}\bigcup_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{i}}C
, one has

\Pr_{(L,L^{\prime})\in E({\sf Grass}(n,\ell))}\left[L,L^{\prime}\in S\land(L\not\in\tilde{S}\text{ or }L^{\prime}\not\in\tilde{S})\right]\leq\eta\delta(S).

3.

For all
j<i
,
C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}
and

C_{j}\in\mathcal{C}_{j}
such that
C_{j}\supseteq C_{i}

it holds that
\delta(S\cap C_{j})\leq{\varepsilon}_{j}\delta(C_{j})
.

Proof sketch.

We let
{\varepsilon}_{0},\ldots,{\varepsilon}_{r}
to be determined; it will only
matter to us that
{\varepsilon}_{r}
is small enough.
Take
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}
to be all
C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}

such that
\delta(S\cap C_{i})\geq{\varepsilon}_{i}\delta(C_{i})
and
for which there are no
j
and
C_{j}\in\mathcal{C}_{j}

such that
C_{j}\supseteq C_{i}
and
\delta(S\cap C_{j})\geq{\varepsilon}_{j}\delta(S)
.

Let
F=1_{S}
,
\tilde{F}=1_{\tilde{S}}
,
and note that
G=F(1-\tilde{F})=1_{S\cap\overline{\tilde{S}}}

is
(r,{\varepsilon}_{r})
pseudo-random as per the definition of
[
KMS18
]
, so
\Phi(S\cap\overline{\tilde{S}})\geq 1-\eta^{2}/100
provided that
{\varepsilon}_{r}
is sufficiently small. Note that

\Pr_{(L,L^{\prime})\in E({\sf Grass}(n,\ell))}\left[L,L^{\prime}\in S\land(L\not\in\tilde{S}\text{ or }L^{\prime}\not\in\tilde{S})\right]\leq 2\E_{(L,L^{\prime})}[F[L]F[L^{\prime}](1-\tilde{F}[L])],

which is equal to
2\langle G,TF\rangle
, where

T
is the adjacency operator of the Grassmann graph with
self loops.
We may bound:

\displaystyle\langle G,TF\rangle=\langle TG,F\rangle\leq\|TG\|_{2}\|F\|_{2}=\sqrt{\langle TG,TG\rangle}\|F\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\langle G,TG\rangle}\|F\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\|G\|_{2}\sqrt{1-\Phi(S\cap\overline{\tilde{S}})}\|F\|_{2}.

We used the fact that
T
is positive semi-definite.
Clearly
\|G\|_{2},\|F\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\delta(S)}
hence we get

\langle G,TF\rangle\leq\delta(S)\frac{\eta}{10}
,
and plugging that above gives the second item.
∎

Adjusting this result to the setting of the shift
partition and phrasing it as an SoS statement
in the standard way, one gets an analog of
Theorem
4.3
for the
Grassmann graph. For that, one has to address
edges going across different
C_{i}
’s and
show that they have a small contribution.
For that one has to choose

{\varepsilon}_{i}
to be sufficiently smaller than

{\varepsilon}_{i+1}
, and include in
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{\prime}

only
C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
that are maximally dense, and repeat a calculation analogous to the one in Theorem
4.3
when bounding the last term therein.

The rest of our algorithm then proceeds in exactly the same way.

5.3
The algorithm for High Dimensional Expanders

In this section we consider UG instances defined over higher-order walks on two-sided local spectral expanders and discuss why our results for Johnson graphs (that can be seen as partial-swap walks over the complete complex) generalize to HDXs. We refer the reader to
[
DDFH18
]
for an excellent exposition on HDXs.

For concreteness, let us consider a consider a two-sided local spectral expanding complex
X
and the partial-swap walk over
X(\ell)
with depth
\alpha
. This random-walk naturally corresponds to a graph
G
over the vertices
X(\ell)
. Given
G
, analogous to the subcubes in the Johnson graph, there are a family of basic sets called the links of the complex.
[
BHKL22b
]
,
[
GLL22
]
generalized the result of
[
KMMS18
]
to prove global hypercontractivity on HDXs and in particular showed that any non-expanding set in
G
must have large constant density inside a link. The parameters they get are the same as that in the statement of
[
KMMS18
]
for the
\alpha
-noisy Johnson graphs. In fact the proof of
[
BHKL22b
]
proceeded exactly along the lines of
[
KMMS18
]
and therefore can be easily seen to be SoS-able to get an analogue of Theorem
3.17
for HDXs. One can similarly also get the stronger edge-covering theorem for HDXs (analogous to Theorem
4.3
) and we omit the details here.

Fortunately for us,
[
BHKL22a
]
generalized the algorithm of
[
BBK+21
]
to get a UG algorithm for HDXs albeit suffered the same two drawbacks as
[
BBK+21
]
– an
\ell
-dependent soundness guarantee and reliance on completeness being close to
1
.
[
BHKL22a
]
thus show the generality of the
[
BBK+21
]
framework and illustrate that it does not depend on the symmetry properties/regularity of Johnson graphs, since higher-order walks could be over irregular sparse and highly asymmetric graphs. We can similarly generalize the Johnson result to get an algorithm for UG on HDXs via the steps outlined in Section
5.1
. To summarize, component 1 therein – an SoS certificate of global hypercontractivity for HDXs – can be obtained using
[
BHKL22b
]
, components 2 and 3 work for all graphs, and component 4 is an iteration result that we can obtain, akin to the iteration result in
[
BHKL22a
]
.

Appendix A
Proof of the Iteration Lemma

We give a proof of the iteration lemma. Let
\mathrm{val}_{f}(H)
denote the fraction of edges inside
H
that are satisfied by the assignment
f
.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
c,\gamma,\delta\in(0,1]
and
r\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
I
be an affine UG instance on alphabet
\Sigma
on
J_{n,\ell,\alpha}
with
\ell,n
large enough and value at least
c
.
Suppose we have a subroutine
\mathcal{A}
which given as input any affine UG instance
I^{\prime}
on
J_{n,\ell,\alpha}
with
\mathrm{val}_{\mu}(I^{\prime})\geq c-\gamma
, returns an
\leq r
-restricted subcube
H
on
J
and a partial assignment
f
such that,
\text{val}_{f}(H)\geq\delta
.
Then if
\mathcal{A}
runs in time
T(\mathcal{A})
, there is a
O(|V(J)|T(\mathcal{A})+|V(J)|^{3})
-time algorithm which finds a solution for
I
that satisfies an
\Omega(\delta\gamma(1-\alpha)^{r})
-fraction of the edges of
J
.

Proof.

We will use the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
as a subroutine. To get a full assignment, our algorithm below is a generalized version of the Algorithm
3.4
, where we’ve replaced some of the steps in Algorithm
3.4
with an arbitrary subroutine
\mathcal{A}
that finds a subcube and an assignment to it with high value. We include it here for completeness.

Algorithm A.2

(Partial to Full Assignment)
.

1.

Set
j=1,I_{0}=I,R_{0}=\phi
.

2.

While
|R_{j-1}|<\frac{\gamma}{2}|V(J)|
:

(a)

Run subroutine
\mathcal{A}
on
I_{j-1}
to find an
\leq r
-restricted subcube
H_{j}
and partial assignment
f_{j}
.

(b)

Let
S_{j}
be the induced subgraph of
H_{j}
induced by the set
H_{j}\setminus R_{j-1}
and assign them using
f_{j}
. Let
R_{j}=R_{j-1}\cup H_{j}
.

(c)

Randomize the constraints on edges incident on
R_{j}
and let the new instance by
I_{j}
.

(d)

Increment
j
.

3.

Output any assignment to
V(G)
that agrees with all partial assignments
f_{j}
(assigned to
S_{j}
’s) considered above.

Let us analyse the
j^{th}
-iteration of this procedure. We are given an instance
I_{j-1}
.

Claim A.3
.

\mathrm{val}(I_{j-1})\geq\mathrm{val}(I)-\frac{2|V(R_{j-1})|}{|V(G)|}
.

Proof.

Suppose
X
is an assignment that achieves the value of
I
. We can check that
X
satisfies at least
\mathrm{val}(I)-\frac{2|V(R_{j-1})|}{|V(G)|}
-fraction of edges of
I_{j-1}
, hence the claim follows. To see this note that the fraction of edges incident on
R_{j-1}
and therefore the fraction that is randomized is at most
\frac{2|V(R_{j-1})|}{|V(G)|}
and every edge not incident on
R_{j-1}
remains satisfied by
X
.
∎

We will show that the partial assignment
f_{j}
to
H_{j}\setminus R_{j-1}
satisfies a large fraction of edges (as counted in the whole graph). Before that let us show that the randomization of edges ensures that the value of the edges randomized is small (even when restricted to some subcube). Let
E(S)
denote the edges incident on
S
(including those with only one endpoint in
S
) and let
E(S,S)
denote the edges with both endpoints in
S
. For a set of edges
E
let
\mathrm{val}_{f}(E)
denote the fraction of edges in
E
that are satisfied by
f
.

Claim A.4
.

With probability
1-o(1)
the instance
I_{j-1}
satisfies the following for all
r
-restricted subcubes
C
:

\mathrm{val}(E(C\cap R_{j-1}))\leq\frac{2}{|\Sigma|},

where
\mathrm{val}(E(S))
denotes the maximum fraction of
E(S)
that can be satisfied by any UG assignment to the endpoints of
E(S)
.

Proof.

Let
k
denote
|\Sigma|
. Fix an
i
-restricted subcube
C
for
i\leq r
. We know that
R_{j-1}
is a union of
\leq r
-restricted subcubes, therefore
C\cap R_{j-1}
also forms a union of
\leq r
-restricted subcubes inside
C
. Let this collection of subcubes be denoted by
\mathcal{C}
.
Consider the set of edges
E
inside
C
on which the constraints were randomized (in the previous iteration), and let
A
be the set of vertices in
C
that are endpoints of them. We claim that with probability
1-o(1)
, for all assignments
F
to
A
,
F
satisfies at most
2/k
of the constraints. First, the number of assignments is
k^{|A|}
, so we fix one assignment
F
, analyze it, and then union bound over all assignments.

Fix
F
; for each
e\in E
, the probability that
F
satisfies
e
is
1/k
. Hence in expectation
F
satisfies

\mu=1/k
fraction of the constraints, and by Chernoff’s bound

\Pr[\mathrm{val}_{F}(E)\geq 2\mu]\leq e^{-\frac{|E|}{3k}}.

Now we lower bound
|E|
. Note that if
v\in A
, then either
v
was in a subcube in
\mathcal{C}
or
v
was adjacent to a vertex in a subcube from
\mathcal{C}
. Let the degree of
v
inside
C
be denoted by
d
which we know is
{\ell-i\choose\alpha(\ell-i)}{n-\ell\choose\alpha(\ell-i)}
.

1.

If
v\in C^{\prime}
for some
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
then at least
(1-\alpha)^{r}
of the edges incident to
v
remain in
C^{\prime}
and therefore are in
E
.

2.

If
v
is adjacent to
u\in R_{j-1}\cap C
then
u\in C^{\prime}
for some
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
. In particular there is a subset
I\subseteq u
of size at most
r
such that
C^{\prime}
is an
I
-restricted subcube. It follows that in fact
v
has at least
{\ell-i\choose\alpha(\ell-i)}{n-\ell-r\choose\alpha(\ell-i)–r}
neighbours in
C^{\prime}
. By a simple calculation this is at least
n^{-r}d
, and it follows that at least
n^{-r}d
of the edges adjacent to
v
are in
E
.

In conclusion, in any case we get that at least
n^{-r}d
of the edges adjacent to
v\in A
are inside
E
, hence
|E|\geq n^{-r}d|A|/2\gg 3n|\Sigma||A|
, so Chernoff gives a probability of
e^{-n|\Sigma||A|}
which is good enough for
a union bound over all (
k^{|A|}
many) assignments to
A
. Additionally we union bound over all
\leq r
-restricted subcubes
C
which are only
n^{O(r)}
many, to get the statement of the lemma.
∎

At iteration
j
of the while-loop, since the while condition is met, we know that
I_{j-1}
has value
\geq c-\gamma
(Claim
A.3
) so inside the while-loop,
\mathcal{A}
will always find an
\leq r
-subcube
H_{j}
and assignment
f_{j}
with
\mathrm{val}_{f_{j}}(H_{j})\geq\delta
. Next, we find an assignment
f_{j}
to the set of vertices
V(S_{j})
that by definition don’t intersect previously assigned vertices. Since
f_{j}
doesn’t reassign any vertices, in the final step of the algorithm it is possible to output an assignment that is consistent with all previously considered partial assignments. We will now show that our final partial assignment satisfies a large fraction of the edges, where we say that an edge
(u,v)
is satisfied by a partial assignment
f_{j}
, if both vertices
u,v
have been assigned labels under
f_{j}
and the labels satisfy the edge.

Claim A.5
.

The value of the partial assignment found at iteration
j
satisfies:

\mathrm{val}(f_{j})\geq\delta(1-\alpha)^{r}\frac{|V(H_{j})|}{2|V(G)|},

where
\mathrm{val}(f_{j})
denotes the fraction of edges (in
E(G)
) satisfied by the partial assignment
f_{j}
.

Proof.

Let
H_{j}=S_{j}\cup(H_{j}\cap R_{j-1})
and let
E_{1}=E(S_{j},S_{j})
and
E_{2}=E(H_{j}\cap R_{j-1})
so that
E_{1}\cup E_{2}=E(H_{j},H_{j})
denoted by
E
. By Claim
A.4
we know that
\mathrm{val}_{f_{j}}(E_{2})\leq 2/k
where
k
denotes
|\Sigma|
.
By an averaging argument:

\delta\leq\frac{\#\text{sat}_{f_{j}}(E_{1})}{|E|}+\frac{|E_{2}|}{|E|}\mathrm{val}_{f_{j}}(E_{2})\leq\frac{\#\text{sat}_{f_{j}}(E_{1})}{|E|}+\frac{2}{k},

where
\#\text{sat}_{f_{j}}(E_{1})
denotes the number of edges of
E_{1}
satisfied by
f_{j}
. This implies that
\frac{\#\text{sat}_{f_{j}}(E_{1})}{|E|}\geq\delta/2
.

Since
H_{j}
is a
\leq r
-restricted subcube at least
(1-\alpha)^{r}
-fraction of edges stay inside
H_{j}
, hence
|E|/|E(H_{j})|
is at least
(1-\alpha)^{r}
. Using that
|E(H_{j})|/|E(J)|\geq|V(H_{j})|/|V(J)|
we get that:

\mathrm{val}(f_{j})=\frac{\#\text{sat}_{f_{j}}(E_{1})}{|E|}\cdot\frac{|E|}{|E(H_{j})|}\cdot\frac{|E(H_{j})|}{|E(J)|}\geq\frac{\delta}{2}(1-\alpha)^{r}\frac{|V(H_{j})|}{|V(G)|},

where
\mathrm{val}(f_{j})
denotes the edges satisfied by
f_{j}
(assigned to
S_{j}
) as a fraction in the whole graph.
∎

Once we have these facts, the conclusion is immediate. Firstly there cannot be more than
V(G)
iterations of the while-loop, since at each iteration we assign at least one new vertex. Each iteration takes time
T(\mathcal{A})+|V(G)|^{2}
, hence the algorithm runs in time
|V(G)|(T(\mathcal{A})+|V(G)|^{2})
.

Suppose the algorithm exits the while loop at the
t+1
iteration. Then we know that
R_{t}\geq\frac{\gamma}{2}|V(J)|
.
Using claims
A.5
above, we get that the value of the final assignment is proportional to the number of vertices assigned:

\sum_{j=1}^{t}\mathrm{val}(f_{j})\geq\delta(1-\alpha)^{r}\sum_{j=1}^{t}\frac{|V(H_{j})|}{2|V(J)|}\geq\delta(1-\alpha)^{r}\frac{|R_{t}|}{2|V(J)|}\geq\delta(1-\alpha)^{r}\frac{\gamma}{4},

thus proving the lemma.
∎

Appendix B
Missing Proofs

The following claim bounds the Booleanity error term
and shows that there is a SoS proof that they are
negligible (provided that we take the parameter
\nu

to be sufficiently small).

Claim B.1
.

\mathcal{A}_{I}~~\vdash_{\mathrm{poly}(\ell^{r}/\nu)}~~\E_{c}[\sum_{s}B(H^{c}_{s})]\leq|\Sigma|\ell^{O(r)}\sqrt{\nu}.

Proof.

Recall that
B(F)=\frac{4}{3}\E_{u}[(F(u)^{3}-F(u))(\Pi_{\geq\lambda_{r}}F)(u)]=\frac{4}{3}\langle F^{3}-F,PF\rangle
, where
\Pi
is the projection operator
\Pi_{\geq\lambda_{r}}
. Using Cauchy-Schwarz we get that for all
\eta>0
:

B(F)\leq\frac{4}{3}\left(\frac{\eta}{2}||F^{3}-F||_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\eta}||\Pi F||_{2}^{2}\right)\leq\frac{2\eta}{3}\E_{u}[F(u)-F(u)^{3}]+\frac{2}{3\eta}\delta(F^{2}),

(40)

where the last inequality holds since
F\in[0,1]
and
\Pi
is a projection operator. We will use this
to bound
B(H_{s}^{c})
as follows.
Fixing
s,u
, taking
F=H_{s}^{c}(u)
,
the first term above is

\E_{c}[(H_{s}^{c}(u)-H_{s}^{c}(u)^{3}]
and we upper bound it
by
O(\ell^{r}\nu)
. Indeed, first as

0\leq H_{s}^{c}(u)\leq 1
we have

\E_{c}[H_{s}^{c}(u)-H_{s}^{c}(u)^{3}]=(1+H_{s}^{c}(u))\E_{c}[H_{s}^{c}(u)-H_{s}^{c}(u)^{2}]\leq 2\E_{c}[H_{s}^{c}(u)-H_{s}^{c}(u)^{2}].

(41)

Next, recall that
H_{s}^{c}(u)
is a product of the terms
(1-R^{c}_{s,a})
for
a\subset u,|a|\leq r
. Additionally each variable
R^{c}_{s,a}
is a product of approximate indicators: namely there are
\beta_{i}\in(0,1)
such that
R_{s,a}^{c}(u)=p^{c,s,a}_{1}(\delta^{c,s,a}_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot p^{c,s,a}_{k}(\delta^{c,s,a}_{k})
, where
p^{c,s,a}_{i}=p_{\beta_{i}+c\nu^{2},\nu^{2}}
or
p_{<\beta_{i}+c\nu^{2},\nu^{2}}
as
c
varies in
\{0,1,\ldots,\lfloor 1/\nu\rfloor\}
, and
\delta_{i}
’s are linear functions of the variables
G_{s}(u)
and lie in
[0,1]
under the axioms
\mathcal{A}_{I}
.
By Claim
B.2
(applied twice) it follows that:

\E_{c}[H_{s}^{c}(u)-H_{s}^{c}(u)^{2}]\leq\sum_{a\subset u:|a|\leq r}\E_{c}[R^{c}_{s,a}-(R^{c}_{s,a})^{2}]\leq\sum_{a\subset u:|a|\leq r}\sum_{i\in[k]}\E_{c}[p_{i}^{c,s,a}(\delta^{c,s,a}_{i})-p_{i}^{c,s,a}(\delta^{c,s,a}_{i})^{2}],

so we can bound each of the terms above.

We will show that each term is smaller than
O(\nu)
. Let
p
be an approximate indicator polynomial
p=p_{\beta,\nu}
and let
p^{c}
denote
p_{\beta+c\nu^{2},\nu^{2}}
for
c\in\{0,\ldots,\lfloor 1/\nu\rfloor\}
. For all
\delta\in[0,1]
, we argue that

\delta\in[0,1]\vdash_{\widetilde{O}(1/\nu^{2})}~~\E_{c}[p^{c}(\delta)-p^{c}(\delta)^{2}]\leq O(\nu).

(42)

Indeed, this follows by case analysis on
\delta\in[0,1]
(that we show next), and then using Lúkacs theorem (Corollary
2.5
) we get there is an SoS proof of degree
\deg(p^{c})
.

If
\delta\in[0,\beta]\cup[\beta+\nu,1]
then one can check that
p^{c}(\delta)\in[0,\nu^{2}]
for all
c
. Therefore let
\delta\in[\beta,\beta+\nu]
, and specifically in one of the intervals -
[\beta+t\nu^{2},\beta+(t+1)\nu^{2}]
for
t\in\{0,\ldots,\lfloor 1/\nu\rfloor\}
. Then we get that
p^{t}(\delta)
could be any number between
[0,1]
and therefore
p^{t}(\delta)-p^{t}(\delta)^{2}\leq 1/4
. But for every
t^{\prime}\neq t
we get that
p^{t^{\prime}}(\delta)
is either in the interval
[0,\nu^{2}]
or
[1-\nu^{2},1]
implying that
p^{t^{\prime}}(\delta)-p^{t^{\prime}}(\delta)^{2}\leq O(\nu^{2})
. Averaging over
a
we therefore get:

\E[p^{c}(\delta)-p^{c}(\delta)^{2}]\leq\nu\cdot\frac{1}{4}+(1-\nu)O(\nu^{2})\leq O(\nu).

In conclusion, we get that

\E_{c}[H_{s}^{c}(u)-H_{s}^{c}(u)^{2}]\leq O(k\ell^{r}\nu)=\ell^{O(r)}\nu
. Plugging this into (
40
)
and taking
\eta=1/\sqrt{\nu}
we get that

\E_{c}[B(H^{c}_{s})]\leq\sqrt{\nu}\ell^{O(r)}+O(\sqrt{\nu}\E_{c}[\delta(H^{c}_{s})])\leq\sqrt{\nu}\ell^{O(r)},

and summing over
s\in\Sigma
finishes the proof.
∎

Claim B.2
.

\{0\leq x_{i}\leq 1\}\vdash_{2k}\prod_{i\in[k]}x_{i}-(\prod_{i\in[k]}x_{i})^{2}\leq x_{1}-x_{1}^{2}+\ldots+x_{k}-x_{k}^{2}.

Proof.

We prove this by induction. For
k=1
this is clear, and for
k=2
we have

\displaystyle xy-(xy)^{2}

\displaystyle=xy–xy^{2}+xy^{2}–x^{2}y^{2}

\displaystyle=x(y-y^{2})+(x-x^{2})y^{2}

\displaystyle\leq y-y^{2}+x-x^{2}.

For the inductive step, let
k\geq 3
and denote
y=x_{1}\cdots x_{k-1}
. Applying the base case and
then the inductive hypothesis we get

\prod_{i\in[k]}x_{i}-\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}x_{i}\right)^{2}=yx_{k}-(yx_{k})^{2}\leq y-y^{2}+x_{k}-x_{k}^{2}\leq x_{1}-x_{1}^{2}+\ldots+x_{k-1}^{2}-x_{k-1}^{2}.

One can check that the SoS degree used is at most
2k
.
∎

Appendix C
Sum-of-Squares Certificate of Expansion in the Johnson Graph

In this section we give an SoS proof of Theorem
3.17
.
Our proof follows the same lines as
[
KMMS18
]
, except that we implement each step carefully by a low-degree SoS proof. For convenience, we restate
Theorem
3.17
below.

Theorem C.1

(Expansion Theorem
3.17
for Johnson Graphs restated)
.

For all
\alpha\in(0,1)
, all integers
\ell\geq 1/\alpha
and
n\geq\ell
, the following holds: Let
J_{n,\ell,\alpha}
be the
\alpha
-noisy Johnson graph. For every constant
\gamma\in(0,1)
and positive integer
r\leq O(\ell)
, every function
F:V(J)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that is
(r,\gamma)
-pseudorandom has high expansion:

1.

\{F(X)\in[0,1]\}_{X\in V(J)}\vdash_{O(1)}\,\\
\langle F,LF\rangle\geq\delta(1-(1-\alpha)^{r+1})(1-\gamma^{1/3}\exp(r))-\sum_{j=0}^{r}\frac{c_{j}\ell^{j}}{\gamma}\E_{a\sim{[n]\choose j}}[q_{a}(F)({\delta(F^{2}|_{a})-\gamma)}]+B(F)

2.

\{F(X)\in[0,1]\}\,\,\,\vdash_{2}\,\,\,0\leq q_{a}(F)\leq 1,

where for all
j\leq r
,
c_{j}
’s are positive constants of size at most
\exp(r)
, for all

a\subseteq[n]
of size
j
,
q_{a}(F)
is a degree
2
polynomials, and
B(F)=\frac{4}{3}\E_{X}[(F^{3}-F)\Pi_{\geq\lambda_{r}}F]
for
\lambda_{r}=(1-\alpha)^{r}
. Here,
\Pi_{\geq\lambda}
is the projection operator on the space of spanned by eigenvectors of
J
of eigenvalues at least
\lambda
.

It will be more convenient for us to move to a closely related Cayley graph, which we denote by
C_{n,\ell,\alpha}
. This graph is essentially the same as the Johnson graph, albeit viewed as a product domain. In Theorem
C.18
, we state an analogous structure theorem for “permutation-invariant” sets on
C_{n,\ell,\alpha}
that we show in Theorem
C.18
, and it is easy to derive Theorem
C.1
above from it.

Notation.

We use
[n]
to denote the set
\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
, and also the group
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})
, the natural numbers modulo
n
.
Generally, when we take a set
S
and raise it to a positive integer power
\ell
, we mean the set of all ordered multisets of elements of
S
of size
\ell
. We use
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{t}
for
t\in[n]
to denote the characters of the group
\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
(or the eigenvectors of the
n
-cycle), where
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{t}:[n]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is the function
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{t}(x)=e^{\frac{2\pi itx}{n}}
.
We will use
\lambda_{G}(v)
to denote the eigenvalue of
v
which is an eigenvector of the adjacency matrix of graph
G
. For a string
S\in\Sigma^{m}
, for some alphabet
\Sigma
, and a set
I\subseteq[m]
, we denote its restriction to the set of coordinates in
I
, by
S|_{I}
.

C.1
The Graph
C_{n,\ell,\alpha}
and its Spectrum

Definition C.2

(Johnson-approximating Cayley Graph
C_{n,\ell,\alpha}
)
.

Let
\alpha
be a number in
(0,1)
and
\ell
be a positive integer. Let
n
be a positive integer such that
n>\ell
.

1.

The vertex set of
C_{n,\ell,\alpha}
is
[n]^{\ell}
. We will drop the subscript
(n,\ell,\alpha)
in
C_{n,\ell,\alpha}
when these parameters are clear from context.

2.

The edges are described by the following random process. For a vertex
X=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell}),x_{i}\in[n]
, the distribution over the neighbours of
X
is described by: choose
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell})
uniformly at random from
[n]^{\ell}
and
b=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell})\sim\{0,1\}^{\ell}
such that the Hamming weight of
b
equals
\alpha\ell
, and output
Z=(x_{1}+b_{1}\cdot y_{1},\ldots,x_{l}+b_{\ell}\cdot y_{\ell})
as the neighbour of
X
.

It is easy to verify that the graph defined above is a weighted Cayley graph with vertex set being the elements of the group
[n]^{\ell}=(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})^{\ell}
. The natural group operation associated with this set is component-wise addition modulo
n
.

We next discuss the spectral properties of
C_{n,\ell\alpha}
. Overloading notations, we let
C=C_{n,\ell,\alpha}
also denote the normalized adjacency matrix of the graph
C
. Note firstly that the eigenvectors of
C
are the characters of the group
[n]^{\ell}
, namely
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}
where
T=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})\in[n]^{\ell}
defined as
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}(x)=\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T_{1}}(x_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T_{\ell}}(x_{\ell})
for
x\in[n]^{\ell}

We next define the degree of an eigenvector.

Definition C.3

(Degree of
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}
)
.

For all
T\in[n]^{\ell}
, where
T=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
, define the degree of
\chi_{T}
as:

{\sf deg}(\chi_{T})=|T|:=|\{i\mid T_{i}\neq 0\}|.

The following lemma asserts that the eigenvalue of
\chi_{T}
is roughly
(1-\alpha)^{|T|}
:

Lemma C.4
.

Let
\lambda_{C}(\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T})
denote the eigenvalue of
C
corresponding to the eigenvector
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}
for
T\in[n]^{\ell}
. We have that,

\lambda_{C}(\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T})=\begin{cases}\frac{\binom{\ell-|T|}{(1-\alpha)\ell-|T|}}{\binom{\ell}{(1-\alpha)\ell}},~~~~|T|\leq(1-\alpha)\ell\\
0,~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
T=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
. For all
X\in[n]^{\ell}
, we have that,

\displaystyle C\cdot\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}(X)=\E_{y,b}[\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}(x_{1}+b_{1}y,\ldots,x_{l}+b_{l}y_{l})]

\displaystyle=\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}(X)\E_{y,b}[\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T_{1},\ldots,T_{l}}(b_{1}y_{1},\ldots,b_{l}y_{l})]

\displaystyle=\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}(X)\E_{y,b}[\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{(b_{1}T_{1},\ldots,b_{l}T_{l})}(y)].

For
y,S\in[n]
and
S\neq 0
, we know that the eigenvector
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{S}
is orthogonal to the eigenvector
\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{0}
, equivalently that
\E_{y}[\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{S}(y)]=0
, whereas if
S=0
then
\E_{y}[\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{S}(y)]=1
. So we get that,

\displaystyle\lambda_{C}(\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T})=\E_{y,b}[\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{(b_{1}T_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell}T_{\ell})}(y)]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{b}[(b_{1}T_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell}T_{\ell})=0^{\ell}]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{\binom{l-|T|}{(1-\alpha)l-|T|}}{\binom{l}{(1-\alpha)l}},~~~~|T|\leq(1-\alpha)\ell\\
0,~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}.

∎

C.2
Analyzing non-expanding sets of the Johnson graph

Since we want to deal with sets in the Johnson graph
J_{n,\ell,\alpha}
we will only consider “permutation-invariant” sets on
C_{n,\ell,\alpha}
. Notice that the vertices of the Johnson graph are subsets of
[n]
of size
\ell
, whereas the vertices of the Johnson-approximating graph are ordered
\ell
-tuples of
[n]
. Therefore, given a set
S
in the Johnson graph, it has a natural mapping to the set
S^{\prime}
which is a subset of the vertices of the Johnson-approximating graph
C
,

S^{\prime}:=\{(x_{\pi(1)},\ldots,x_{\pi(\ell)})\mid\pi:[\ell]\rightarrow[\ell],\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell}\}\in S\}
. This leads to the following definition:

Definition C.5

(Permutation-invariance)
.

We say that a set
S\subseteq C_{n,\ell,\alpha}
is permutation-invariant if for all permutations
\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{\ell}
and all
X=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})\in S
we have that
X_{\pi}=(x_{\pi(1)},\ldots,x_{\pi(\ell)})
belongs to
S
. Similarly, a function
F:V(C)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is permutation invariant if for all inputs
X=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})
, we have that
F(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})=F(x_{\pi(1)},\ldots,x_{\pi(\ell)})
, for all
\pi\in S_{\ell}
. Further let
\mathcal{A}_{inv}
denote the set of axioms that
F
is permutation-invariant, that is,

\mathcal{A}_{inv}:=\{F(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})=F(x_{\pi(1)},\ldots,x_{\pi(\ell)})\}_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{\ell},X\in[n]^{\ell}}.

Since the set of vertices in
C
that correspond to some set of vertices in
J
are permutation invariant it will be enough to focus are attention on these special sets and from now on whenever we refer to a set in
V(C)
, the reader can assume that it is permutation-invariant.

To analyze non-expanding sets of
C
, we will consider permutation-invariant functions
F:V(C)\rightarrow[0,1]
. Typically one would consider
0/1
-valued functions
F
, where
F
is the indicator function of a set
S
, i.e.
F(X)=1
when
X\in S
. But since we need to analyze “approximate-sets” (the indicator function is approximated by a polynomial that takes values close to
0/1
),
F(X)
could take any value between
[0,1]
.

Recall that the Fourier decomposition of
F
gives us that,
F(X)=\sum_{T}\widehat{F}(T)\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}(X)
. We will now define the following for a function
F
:

Definition C.6
.

Given a function
F\colon V(C)\to[0,1]
, we have the following level decomposition:

1.

We write

F=F_{0}+\ldots+F_{\ell}
,
where
F_{i}(X)=\sum_{T:|T|=i}\widehat{F}(T)\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}(X)
. We will call
F
a level
i
function, if its Fourier decomposition has degree
i
characters only, i.e.
\widehat{F}(T)=0,
for all
T
such that
|T|\neq i
.

2.

Let
f_{i,F}:[n]^{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a function defined as,

f_{i,F}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i}):=\sum_{T_{1},\ldots,T_{i}\in([n]\setminus 0)^{i}}\widehat{F}(T_{1},\ldots,T_{i},0,\ldots)\chi_{T_{1},\ldots,T_{i}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i}),

Let
X=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{j})\in[n]^{j}
and
I
be a subset of
\{1,\ldots,j\}
. Let
I=\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{|I|}\}
where
k_{1}<k_{2}<\ldots<k_{|I|}
. We will use
X|_{I}
to denote the ordered tuple of elements
(x_{k_{1}},\ldots,x_{k_{|I|}})
. We will now state some simple properties of
F
that are implied by permutation-invariance.

Lemma C.7
.

For all functions
F:[n]^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that are permutation-invariant, we have that:

1.

\widehat{F}(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})=\widehat{F}(T_{\pi(1)},\ldots,T_{\pi(\ell)})
, for all
(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})\in[n]^{\ell}
and all permutations
\pi:[\ell]\rightarrow[\ell]
.

2.

The functions
F_{i}
and
f_{i,F}
are also permutation-invariant.

3.

F_{i}(X)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subseteq[\ell]\\
|I|=i\end{subarray}}f_{i,F}(X|_{I})
.

Proof.

A straightforward manipulation of the definitions.
∎

We note that the Fourier coefficients
\widehat{F}(T),\forall T\in(\{0,1\}^{k})^{\ell}
, the level-
i
functions
F_{i}(X)
and
f_{i}(X)
are all linear functions of the indeterminates
\{F(X)\}
,
hence we shall also think of them as indeterminates
when arguing about SoS proofs.

C.3
Restrictions

In this section, we define restrictions of functions
F
and state several lemmas related to them.

Definition C.8

(
r
-restricted subcubes of
C
)
.

Given an ordered tuple,
A=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r})
for
a_{i}\in[n]
and
r\leq\ell-1
, we let
C|_{A}
denote the subset of vertices of
C
whose first
r
coordinates are restricted to be
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r})
. We call such a subset an
r
-restricted subcube of
C
.

Definition C.9

(Restrictions)
.

Given a function
F:[n]^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and an ordered tuple,
A=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r})
for
a_{i}\in[n]
and
1\leq r\leq\ell-1
, we define the restricted function
F|_{A}:[n]^{\ell-r}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as,

F|_{A}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-r})=F(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r},x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-r}).

Further, let
\delta_{A}(F)
denote the mass of the function restricted to
A
, that is,

\delta_{A}(F):=\delta(F|_{A})=\E\limits_{X\in[n]^{\ell-r}}[F|_{A}(X)].

For convenience, when
A=\phi
(
r=0
), define
F|_{A}(X):=F(X)
, and
\delta_{A}(F):=\delta(F)=\E_{X\in[n]^{\ell}}[F(X)]
.

The following lemma gives a relation between the level functions of a function
F
and the level function of its restrictions.

Lemma C.10
.

Let
F
be a permutation-invariant function on
V(C)
. Then we have the following:

1.

For all
a\in[n]
and for all
i
such that
0\leq i\leq\ell-1
, and all
X\in[n]^{i}
, we have that,

f_{i+1,F}(a,X)=f_{i,F|_{\{a\}}}(X)-f_{i,F}(X).

2.

For all integers
i
such that
0\leq i\leq\ell
and for all
X\in[n]^{i}
, we get an inclusion-exclusion formula for
f_{i}(X)
in terms of restrictions of
F
:

f_{i,F}(X)=\sum_{B\subseteq\{1,\ldots,i\}}(-1)^{i-|B|}\delta_{X|_{B}}(F),

where
X|_{B}
is the ordered tuple of elements of
X
restricted to the indices in
B
.

Proof.

We prove each item separately.

Proof of the first item.

Using the definition, we can expand out
f_{i+1,F}
to get that,

f_{i,F}(a,X)=\sum_{(T_{1},\ldots,T_{i+1})\in([n]\setminus 0)^{i+1}}\widehat{F}(T_{1},\ldots,T_{i+1},0,\ldots,0)\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T_{1},\ldots,T_{i+1}}(a,X).

We can split this sum into two parts, one where
T_{1}
can take
any
value (even
0
) and the
second where
T_{1}=0
. We get that,

\displaystyle f_{i,F}(a,X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{1}\in[n]\\
T\in([n]\setminus 0)^{i}\end{subarray}}\widehat{F}(T_{1},T,0,\ldots,0,\ldots,0)\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T_{1},T}(a,X)-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{1}=0\\
T\in([n]\setminus 0)^{i}\end{subarray}}\widehat{F}(0,T,0,\ldots,0)\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}(X).

We will show that the first term equals
f_{i,F|_{\{a\}}}(X)
and the second term equals
f_{i,F}(X)
. This implies the conclusion needed.

For the first term we have that,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{1}\in[n]\\
T\in([n]\setminus 0)^{i}\end{subarray}}\widehat{F}(T_{1},T,0,\ldots,0)\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T_{1},T}(a,X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{1}\in[n]\\
T\in([n]\setminus 0)^{i}\end{subarray}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y_{1}\in[n],\\
Y\in[n]^{\ell-1}\end{subarray}}\left[F(Y_{1},Y)\overline{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T_{1}}}(Y_{1})\overline{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{(T,0,\ldots,0)}(Y)}\right]\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T_{1}}(a)\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}(X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{T\in([n]\setminus 0)^{i}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y_{1}\in[n],\\
Y\in[n]^{\ell-1}\end{subarray}}\left[F(Y_{1},Y)\overline{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{(T,0,\ldots,0)}(Y)}\sum_{T_{1}\in[n]}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T_{1}}(a-Y_{1})\right]\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}(X).

(43)

We now have that
\sum_{T_{1}\in[n]}\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T_{1}}(a-Y_{1})=0
if
Y_{1}\neq a
and equals
n
otherwise. Using this fact we get that (
43
) is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{T\in([n]\setminus 0)^{i}}\frac{1}{n}\cdot\E_{Y\in[n]^{\ell-1}}\left[F(a,Y)\overline{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{(T,0,\ldots,0)}(Y)}\cdot n\right]\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}(X)=\sum_{T\in([n]\setminus 0)^{i}}\widehat{F|_{\{a\}}}(T,0,\ldots,0)\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T}(X),

which is equal to
f_{i,F|_{\{a\}}}(X)
by definition.

As for the second term, it is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}T_{1}=0\\
(T_{2},\ldots,T_{i+1})\in([n]\setminus 0)^{i}\end{subarray}}\widehat{F}(0,T_{2},\ldots,T_{i+1},0,\ldots,0)\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T_{2},\ldots,T_{i+1}}(X)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(T_{2},\ldots,T_{i+1})\in([n]\setminus 0)^{i}}\widehat{F}(T_{2},\ldots,T_{i+1},0,\ldots,0)\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\chi$}_{T_{2},\ldots,T_{i+1}}(X),

since by Lemma
C.7
(1) we have that
\widehat{F}(0,T_{2},\ldots,T_{i+1},0,\ldots)=\widehat{F}(0,T_{2},\ldots,T_{i+1},0,\ldots)
. Since the last equality is the definition of
f_{i,F}(X)
, the conclusion follows.

Proof of the second item.

We will prove this claim by induction on
i
. For the base case of
i=0
, by definition, we have that,

f_{0,F}(\phi)=\widehat{F}(0,\ldots,0)=\E_{X\in[n]^{\ell}}[F(X)]=\delta(F)=\delta_{\phi}(F)=\sum_{B\subseteq\phi}\delta_{\phi|_{B}}(F).

Now let us assume that for all permutation-invariant functions
G
the claim holds for
i-1
, i.e. for all
X\in[n^{i-1}]
, we have that
f_{i-1,G}(X)=\sum_{B\in\{1,\ldots,i-1\}}(-1)^{i-1-|B|}\delta_{X|_{B}}(G)
. Now we will prove the claim for
f_{i,F}
, thus completing the induction.
Let
X=(x_{1},X^{\prime})
, where
X\in[n]^{i},x_{1}\in[n]
and
X^{\prime}\in[n]^{i-1}
. Then by property (1) of the same lemma, we have that,

f_{i,F}(X)=f_{i,F|_{\{x_{1}\}}}(X^{\prime})-f_{i,F}(X^{\prime}).

Expanding the RHS using the induction hypothesis on the functions
F|_{\{x_{1}\}}
and
F
, we get that,

\displaystyle f_{i,F}(X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{B^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,i-1\}}(-1)^{i-1-|B^{\prime}|}\delta_{X^{\prime}|_{B^{\prime}}}(F|_{\{x_{1}\}})-\sum_{B^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,i-1\}}(-1)^{i-1-|B^{\prime}|}\delta_{X^{\prime}|_{B^{\prime}}}(F)

\displaystyle=\sum_{B^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,i-1\}}(-1)^{i-(1+|B^{\prime}|)}\delta_{(x_{1},X^{\prime}|_{B^{\prime}})}(F)+\sum_{B^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,i-1\}}(-1)^{i-|B^{\prime}|}\delta_{X^{\prime}|_{B^{\prime}}}(F)

\displaystyle=\sum_{B\in\{1,\ldots,i\}:1\in B}(-1)^{i-|B|}\delta_{X|_{B}}(F)+\sum_{B\in\{1,\ldots,i\}:1\notin B}(-1)^{i-|B|}\delta_{X|_{B}}(F)

\displaystyle=\sum_{B\in\{1,\ldots,i\}}(-1)^{i-|B|}\delta_{X|_{B}}(F).

This completes the inductive step and the proof of the lemma.
∎

C.4
Calculating
2
-nd Moments

Let
W_{i}(F)
denote the weight of
F
on the
i^{th}
level, that is, let
\eta_{i}=W_{i}[F]=\E_{X}[F_{i}(X)^{2}]
. Let
\delta(F)
denote
\E_{X}[F(X)]
. Now we will derive some lemmas about the second moments. We get the following relation between the level-
i
Fourier weight
\eta_{i}
and the
\ell_{2}
-norm of the
f_{i}
’s.

Lemma C.11
.

For all
i\in[\ell]
, given the variables
\{F(X)\}_{X\in V(C)}
:

\mathcal{A}_{inv}~~\vdash_{2}~~\E_{X\in[n]^{i}}[f_{i,F}(X)^{2}]=\frac{\eta_{i}}{\binom{\ell}{i}}.

Proof.

Since
F_{i}(A)=\sum_{I\subseteq[\ell]}f_{i}(A|_{I})
, we have that,

\displaystyle\eta_{i}=\E_{A\sim[n]^{\ell}}[F_{i}(A)^{2}]=\E_{A}[(\sum_{I}f_{i}(A_{I}))^{2}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{I}\E_{A}[f_{i}(A_{I})^{2}]+\sum_{I\neq I^{\prime}}\E_{A}[f_{i}(A_{I})f_{i}(A_{I^{\prime}})]

\displaystyle=\sum_{I}\E_{A}[f_{i}(A_{I})^{2}]+0

\displaystyle=\E_{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i})}[f_{i}(X)^{2}]\cdot\binom{\ell}{i},

and rearranging implies the lemma.
∎

Lemma C.12
.

For all
i\in[\ell]
, for all
a,b\in[i]
, for all
A\in(\{0,1\}^{k})^{a}
, given the indeterminates
\{F(X)\}_{X\in V(C)}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{inv}(F)~~~\vdash_{2}~~~

\displaystyle\E_{X\in(\{0,1\}^{k})^{i-a}}[f_{i}(A,X)^{2}]\leq\frac{\delta(F^{2}|_{A})}{{l-a\choose i-a}}.

Proof.

By Lemma
C.11
, we have that,

\displaystyle\E_{X\in(\{0,1\}^{k})^{i-a}}[f_{i}(A,X)^{2}]=\E_{X\sim(\{0,1\}^{k})^{i-a}}[f_{i-a,F|_{A}}(B)^{2}]=\frac{W^{i-a}(F|_{A})}{{l-a\choose i-a}}.

(44)

We also have that,

\displaystyle W^{i-a}(F|_{A})\leq\E_{X\in(\{0,1\}^{k})^{i-a}}[F|_{A}(X)^{2}]=\delta(F^{2}|_{A}),

(45)

where the first inequality is a degree 2 SoS-inequality. The proof is concluded by combining (
44
), (
45
).
∎

C.5
Bounding
4
-th moments

The next part of the proof is to prove a lower
bound on the fourth moment of
F_{i}
(using the fact
it is correlated with a Boolean valued function,
hence is supposed to have a high
4
th moment)
as well as an upper bound on the fourth moment of

F_{i}
(using the fact
F
is pseudo-random). We
begin with the following lemma which gives us the lower bound:

Lemma C.13

(Lower Bound)
.

For all real
\epsilon>0
given the indeterminates
\{F(X)\}_{X\in V(C)}
we have that,

\E_{X}[F_{i}^{4}(X)]\geq 4\epsilon^{3}\eta_{i}-3\epsilon^{4}\delta(F)+B(F),

where
B(F)=4{\varepsilon}^{3}\E_{X}[(F^{3}-F)F_{i}]+3{\varepsilon}^{4}\E_{X}[F-F^{4}]
.

Proof.

We have that,

\displaystyle\eta_{i}=\langle F,F_{i}\rangle=\E_{X}[F(X)^{3}\cdot F_{i}(X)]+\E_{X}[(F(X)-F(X)^{3})\cdot F_{i}(X)],

(46)

since
F=\sum F_{i}
is an orthogonal decomposition of
F
. Using Fact
2.4
on the first term we get:

\displaystyle\E_{X}[F(X)^{3}\cdot F_{i}(X)]

\displaystyle=\E_{X}[F(X)^{3}\cdot F_{i}(X)]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3\epsilon}{4}\E_{X}[F(X)^{4}]+\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{3}}\E_{X}[F_{i}(X)^{4}],

\displaystyle=\frac{3\epsilon}{4}\E_{X}[F(X)]+\frac{3\epsilon}{4}\E_{X}[F(X)^{4}-F(X)]+\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{3}}\E_{X}[F_{i}(X)^{4}],

for all real
\epsilon>0
. Plugging this into (
46
) and rearranging gives the lemma.
∎

We will now prove an upper bound on
\E_{X}[F_{i}^{4}(X)]
. We have the following lemma which bounds the fourth moment of
F_{i}
in terms of the second moment of
F_{i}
:

Lemma C.14

(Upper Bound)
.

For all integers
i\in[\ell]
, there exist positive constants
c_{1},\ldots,c_{i}\leq\exp(i)
such that for all
F
:

\mathcal{A}_{inv}\,\,\vdash_{O(1)}\,\,\E_{X}[F_{i}(X)^{4}]\leq\exp(i)\gamma\eta_{i}+\sum_{j=0}^{i}c_{j}\ell^{i}\E_{a\sim[n]^{j}}[\E_{b\sim[n]^{\ell-j}}[f_{i}(a,b)^{2}]\cdot(\delta(F^{2}|_{a})-\gamma)].

Proof.

When the set
X
and the function
F
is clear from context, we use
f_{i}(I)
in place of
f_{i}(X|_{I})
. Thus,

\displaystyle\E_{X}[F_{i}^{4}(X)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I_{1},\ldots,I_{4}\subseteq[l]\\
|I_{j}|=i,\forall j\in[4]\end{subarray}}\E_{X}[f_{i}(I_{1})f_{i}(I_{2})f_{i}(I_{3})f_{i}(I_{4})].

Denote,

D(i,d)=\{(I_{1},\ldots,I_{4})\mid\forall j\in[4],I_{j}\subseteq[l],|I_{j}|=i,|I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup I_{3}\cup I_{4}|=d\}.

Note that for all
d\in[i,4i]
,

|D(i,d)|\leq{\ell\choose d}\cdot{d\choose i}^{4}\leq\left(\frac{\ell e}{d}\right)^{d}\left(\frac{de}{i}\right)^{4i}\leq\ell^{d}\exp(i).

We will prove the following lemma about the four-wise products, for
(I_{1},\ldots,I_{4})\in D(i,d)
. We defer
the proof to Section
C.6
.

Lemma C.15
.

Let
i,d
be integers such that
i\leq d\leq 4i
and let
I_{1},\ldots,I_{4}\subseteq[l]
, such that
(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},I_{4})\in D(i,d)
. Then there exist integers
t_{1},t_{2}\in[i]
such that for all
\gamma
:

\displaystyle\E_{X}[f_{i}(I_{1})f_{i}(I_{2})f_{i}(I_{3})f_{i}(I_{4})]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{O(i)}{\ell}\right)^{d}\gamma\eta_{i}+\left(\frac{O(i)}{\ell}\right)^{d-i}\E_{a_{1}\sim[n]^{t_{1}}}[\E_{b_{1}\sim[n]^{i-t_{1}}}[f_{i}(a_{1},b_{1})^{2}]\cdot(\delta(F^{2}|_{a_{1}})-\gamma)]

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{O(i)}{\ell}\right)^{d-i}\E_{a_{2}\sim[n]^{t_{2}}}[\E_{b_{2}\sim[n]^{i-t_{2}}}[f_{i}(a_{2},b_{2})^{2}](\delta(F^{2}|_{a_{2}})-\gamma)].

We now use Lemma
C.15
to finish the proof of Lemma
C.14
. Indeed, we get that

\displaystyle\E_{X}[F_{i}^{4}(X)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I_{1},\ldots,I_{4}\subseteq[l]\\
|I_{j}|=i,\forall j\in[4]\end{subarray}}\E_{X}[f_{i}(I_{1})f_{i}(I_{2})f_{i}(I_{3})f_{i}(I_{4})]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{d=i}^{4i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I_{1},\ldots,I_{4}\subseteq[l]\\
(I_{1},\ldots,I_{4})\in D(i,d)\end{subarray}}\E_{X}[f_{i}(I_{1})f_{i}(I_{2})f_{i}(I_{3})f_{i}(I_{4})]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{d=i}^{4i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I_{1},\ldots,I_{4}\subseteq[l]\\
(I_{1},\ldots,I_{4})\in D(i,d)\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{O(i)}{\ell}\right)^{d}\gamma\eta_{i}+\left(\frac{O(i)}{\ell}\right)^{d-i}\E_{a_{1}\sim[n]^{t_{1}}}[\E_{b_{1}\sim[n]^{i-t_{1}}}[f_{i}(a_{1},b_{1})^{2}]\cdot(\delta(F^{2}|_{a_{1}})-\gamma)]

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~+\left(\frac{O(i)}{\ell}\right)^{d-i}\E_{a_{2}\sim[n]^{t_{2}}}[\E_{b_{2}\sim[n]^{i-t_{2}}}[f_{i}(a_{2},b_{2})^{2}](\delta(F^{2}|_{a_{2}})-\gamma)]

\displaystyle\leq\exp(i)\gamma\eta_{i}+\sum_{j=0}^{i}c_{j}\ell^{i}\E_{a\sim[n]^{j}}[\E_{b\sim[n]^{i-j}}[f_{i}(a,b)^{2}]\cdot(\delta(F^{2}|_{a})-\gamma)],

for some positive constants
c_{j}\leq\exp(i)
, where we have used that
|D(i,d)|\leq\ell^{d}\exp(i)
.
∎

C.6
Proof of Lemma
C.15

We begin by analyzing the simple case in which there is an index that appears in only one of the
I_{j}
’s,
and show that in this case the expectation is just equal to
0
:

Proposition C.16
.

If there is an index
j
that appears in only one of the
I_{k}
s, then,

\E_{X}[f_{i}(I_{1})f_{i}(I_{2})f_{i}(I_{3})f_{i}(I_{4})]=0.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can assume that
1
appears only in
I_{4}
. We have that,

\displaystyle\E_{X}[f_{i}(I_{1})f_{i}(I_{2})f_{i}(I_{3})f_{i}(I_{4})]

\displaystyle=\E_{x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}}[f_{i}(I_{1})f_{i}(I_{2})f_{i}(I_{3})f_{i}(I_{4})]

\displaystyle=\E_{x_{2},\ldots,x_{l}}[f_{i}(I_{1})f_{i}(I_{2})f_{i}(I_{3})\E_{x_{1}}[f_{i}(I_{4})\mid x_{2}=x_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,x_{l}=x_{l}^{\prime}]]

\displaystyle=\E_{x_{2},\ldots,x_{l}}[f_{i}(I_{1})f_{i}(I_{2})f_{i}(I_{3})\delta_{(x_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,x_{l}^{\prime})\cap I_{4}}(f_{i})]

\displaystyle=0,

where the last equality follows by Lemma
C.7
.
∎

For the rest of the proof we assume each index in
I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup I_{3}\cup I_{4}
appears in at least two sets. Without loss of generality we can assume that
\cup_{j=1}^{4}I_{j}=[d]
. Let
H_{2},H_{3},H_{4}\subseteq[d]
be the set of elements that appear in
2,3,4
of the sets
(I_{1},\ldots,I_{4})
respectively. Furthermore let
H_{2}=X_{12}\cup X_{13}\ldots X_{34},
where
X_{jk}
is the set of elements that occur in
I_{j}
and in
I_{k}
. We will abuse notation and use
H_{j}
to also denote the set of random variables
(x_{j_{1}},\ldots,x_{j_{m}}),m\in H_{j}
and
x_{m}\sim\{0,1\}^{k}
. Firstly we get that,

\displaystyle\E_{H_{2}}[f_{i}(I_{1})f_{i}(I_{2})f_{i}(I_{3})f_{i}(I_{4})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{X_{12},\ldots,X_{34}}[f_{i}(I_{1})f_{i}(I_{2})f_{i}(I_{3})f_{i}(I_{4})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{13},X_{14},\\
X_{23},X_{24}\end{subarray}}[\E_{X_{12}}[f_{i}(I_{1})f_{i}(I_{2})]\E_{X_{34}}[f_{i}(I_{3})f_{i}(I_{4})]]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{13},X_{14},\\
X_{23},X_{24}\end{subarray}}[\E_{X_{12}}[f_{i}(I_{1})f_{i}(I_{2})]^{2}]+\frac{1}{2{\varepsilon}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{13},X_{14},\\
X_{23},X_{24}\end{subarray}}[\E_{X_{34}}[f_{i}(I_{3})f_{i}(I_{4})]^{2}]

(47)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{13},X_{14},\\
X_{23},X_{24}\end{subarray}}[\E_{X_{12}}[f_{i}(I_{1})^{2}]\E_{X_{12}}[f_{i}(I_{2})^{2}]]+\frac{1}{2{\varepsilon}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{13},X_{14},\\
X_{23},X_{24}\end{subarray}}[\E_{X_{34}}[f_{i}(I_{3})^{2}]\E_{X_{34}}[f_{i}(I_{4})^{2}]]

(48)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\E_{X_{12},X_{13},X_{14}}[f_{i}(I_{1})^{2}]\E_{X_{12},X_{23},X_{24}}[f_{i}(I_{2})^{2}]+\frac{1}{2{\varepsilon}}\E_{X_{34},X_{13},X_{23}}[f_{i}(I_{3})^{2}]\E_{X_{34},X_{14},X_{24}}[f_{i}(I_{4})^{2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\E_{H_{2}}[f_{i}(I_{1})^{2}]\E_{H_{2}}[f_{i}(I_{2})^{2}]+\frac{1}{2{\varepsilon}}\E_{H_{2}}[f_{i}(I_{3})^{2}]\E_{H_{2}}[f_{i}(I_{4})^{2}],

(49)

where inequalities (
47
), (
48
) follow by Cauchy Schwarz (Lemma
2.2
and Lemma
2.3
).

We will now bound the expression
\E_{X}[f_{i}(I_{1})f_{i}(I_{2})f_{i}(I_{3})f_{i}(I_{4})]
. We have that,

\displaystyle\E_{X}[f_{i}(I_{1})f_{i}(I_{2})f_{i}(I_{3})f_{i}(I_{4})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{H_{4},H_{3}}[\E_{H_{2}}[f_{i}(I_{1})f_{i}(I_{2})f_{i}(I_{3})f_{i}(I_{4})]]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\E_{H_{4},H_{3}}[\E_{H_{2}}[f_{i}(I_{1})^{2}]\E_{H_{2}}[f_{i}(I_{2})^{2}]]+\frac{1}{2{\varepsilon}}\E_{H_{4},H_{3}}[\E_{H_{2}}[f_{i}(I_{3})^{2}]\E_{H_{2}}[f_{i}(I_{4})^{2}]]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\E_{A}[\E_{B_{1}}[f_{i}(I_{1})^{2}]\E_{B_{2}}[f_{i}(I_{2})^{2}]]+\frac{1}{2{\varepsilon}}\E_{C}[\E_{D_{1}}[f_{i}(I_{3})^{2}]\E_{D_{2}}[f_{i}(I_{4})^{2}]],

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\E_{A}[\E_{B_{1}}[f_{i}(a,b_{1})^{2}]\E_{B_{2}}[f_{i}(a,b_{2})^{2}]]+\frac{1}{2{\varepsilon}}\E_{C}[\E_{D_{1}}[f_{i}(c,d_{1})^{2}]\E_{D_{2}}[f_{i}(c,d_{2})^{2}]],

(50)

with
A=H_{4}\cup(H_{3}\cap I_{1}\cap I_{2})
,

B_{1}=(H_{2}\cap I_{1})\cup((H_{3}\cap I_{1})\setminus I_{2})
,

B_{2}=(H_{2}\cap I_{2})\cup((H_{3}\cap I_{2})\setminus I_{1})
,
C=H_{4}\cup(H_{3}\cap I_{3}\cap I_{4})
,
D_{1}=(H_{2}\cap I_{3})\cup((H_{3}\cap I_{3})\setminus I_{4})
,

D_{2}=(H_{2}\cap I_{4})\cup((H_{3}\cap I_{4})\setminus I_{3})
. The sets
a,b_{1},b_{2},c,d_{1},d_{2}
are instantiations of the corresponding random variables,
A,\ldots,D_{2}
. We have that,

\displaystyle\E_{A}[\E_{B_{1}}[f_{i}(a,b_{1})^{2}]\E_{B_{2}}[f_{i}(a,b_{2})^{2}]]
